At angle 0 it's provably identical to the existing `largestCenteredCropForRatio` (verified both algebraically and with a test asserting exact equality across several ratio/source combinations), so callers don't need an angle-0 special case.
- **Wired into `+page.svelte`'s two crop-reshaping call sites**, not into `cropMath.js`'s lower-level drag/resize helpers (which stay angle-unaware, matching this project's own "fixed, unrotated overlay space" design for the interactive handles): `handleCropChange` now recomputes the rect (same aspect ratio, recentered) whenever an angle-only patch arrives from the straighten slider; `handleCropAspectPreset` now calls the angle-aware function directly instead of the angle-0-only one, so picking a new aspect-ratio preset while already rotated doesn't reintroduce the same bug.
- **Deliberately centered-only, a named scope cut**: an off-center crop's own maximal inscribed bound needs the same per-corner argument repeated at an arbitrary offset — real added complexity the straighten tool doesn't need to solve, since recentering on every angle change is standard crop-tool behavior and covers the common case (the default identity crop is already centered).
- **No Rust or WGSL changes needed** — once the persisted `x/y/width/height` are always inscribed for the persisted `angle`, `apply_crop`/`crop_rect_px` and the WGSL committed-preview math (both of which already rotate about the same image-center pivot, confirmed by reading `rotate_image`'s own doc comment) naturally stop sampling the blanked-out corners, with zero behavior change on their own end.
- **Verification**: 6 new Vitest tests, including a direct empirical check of the actual geometric guarantee — for a spread of source sizes/ratios/angles, every corner of the computed rect is inverse-rotated using the exact same mapping `rotate_image` (Rust) uses and confirmed to land within `[0, sourceWidth] x [0, sourceHeight]` — plus the angle-0 equivalence check, a textbook 45°-square-inscribed-in-square exact-value check (side `1/sqrt(2)`), monotonic shrink with angle, and left/right-angle symmetry. 46/46 Vitest, 192/192 Rust (untouched, run for regression), `npm run check` clean across 239 files. **Not verified this session**: interactively dragging the straighten slider against a real photo in the live GPU window — this environment has no tool that can drive a native macOS window (same documented gap as the Lens Corrections slice above); the fix's correctness instead rests on the geometry being independently, analytically verified against the same rotation formula the Rust backend uses, not just "doesn't crash."

## M3 — Lens Corrections (2026-08-14)

Per an explicit user choice among three offered options: real profile-based correction via the `lensfun` crate (camera+lens EXIF matched against a bundled optical database), not manual-sliders-only or skipping the slice. Mid-slice, the user separately asked for manual (profile-independent) adjustment alongside the profile — resolved by adding Distortion/CA manual sliders but deliberately NOT a manual vignette control (see below).

- **`lensfun` crate v0.7.0** (pure Rust, no C/vcpkg dependency — a deliberate de-risk against this project's existing `rsraw`/LibRaw C dependency's own documented Windows/MSVC build pain). Bundles its lens-correction XML database directly in the binary via `Database::load_bundled()`. License note for later: the code is LGPL-3.0 (fine, repo is open source already), but the upstream lens *database* itself is CC-BY-SA-3.0 — attribution is a real, confirmed obligation, deferred to a future credits/about surface, not blocking this slice.
- **New `lens_profile.rs`** is the ONLY file that touches `lensfun` types — `match_profile(camera_make, camera_model, lens_model, focal_length, aperture)` fuzzy-matches against the bundled database and returns already-*rescaled* distortion/TCA/vignetting coefficients (a direct port of the crate's own private `rescale_*` functions, since they're not public). A new `lookup_lens_profile` Tauri command wraps it; the frontend already holds all five matching inputs in its in-memory `ImageSummary` list, so no new catalog getter was needed.
- **Profile is baked into the op itself, not just an equipment identifier** — `develop_engine.rs`'s `apply_lens_correction`/`export.rs`/`import.rs`'s thumbnail regen have zero dependency on the catalog or per-image metadata, staying pure interpreters of whatever the op JSON says (this codebase's standing Rust-never-interprets-ops-beyond-the-JSON boundary). "Profile goes stale" isn't a reachable risk — EXIF fields in this catalog are immutable, no setter exists.
- **Profile/Manual split**, matching real Lightroom's own tab split: `profile_enabled` + `distortion_amount`/`vignette_amount`/`ca_amount` (0-100, blend the auto-matched profile correction toward identity) vs. `manual_distortion`/`manual_ca` (-100..100, a simple single-coefficient correction in its own normalized space, working even with zero EXIF). **No manual vignette control** — it would be the exact same radial-gain formula the existing creative `vignette` op already exposes; the UI points users there instead of duplicating it.
- **Geometry (distortion + per-channel CA) is a genuine resample, unlike vignetting correction** — each output pixel's R/G/B channels can each sample a different source location, so it needs a fresh output buffer + bilinear sampling (like `apply_crop`'s own rotation), not an in-place per-pixel formula. Vignetting correction is a pure per-pixel gain and runs after, in place. `apply_lens_correction` runs FIRST in both `export.rs` and `import.rs`'s thumbnail regen, before grading and well before crop — the user grades/crops the corrected image, matching real Lightroom.
- **The distortion/TCA formulas are a direct, hand-verified port of `lensfun`'s own pure kernels** (`mod_coord::{undist_poly3,undist_poly5,undist_ptlens}`, `mod_subpix`'s per-channel poly3 inversion) — confirmed by reading the crate's actual vendored source (not just docs.rs prose), which is what revealed the crate exposes small, real, closed-form polynomial coefficients rather than needing a more complex displacement-grid fallback the initial design review considered. Not called through the crate's own `Modifier` type at render time (not `Send`-friendly to hold across renders, and depends on the current render's width/height, so it'd need re-deriving every frame anyway).
- **A real Newton-iteration bug, caught by a hand-derived round-trip test**: the initial poly3 inverse divided the wrong term by `k1` (`ru^3/k1 + ru` instead of `ru^3 + ru/k1`) — a test that computes the forward equation by hand and checks the inverse recovers the original input caught a `0.71` vs. expected `0.4` mismatch immediately. Fixed by re-deriving the algebra from the actual forward equation `k1*ru³+ru-rd=0`. This is exactly why that test shape exists (validates the ported math is actually correct, not just "doesn't crash") — it was NOT flagged by the user, caught and fixed independently.
- **WGSL port for the interactive preview**: a new `fs_lens_correct` pass runs before `fs_grade` inside the existing dirty-key-gated multi-pass block (so a lens-correction-only change re-triggers it, same as texture/clarity's own amounts), writing into a new `lensCorrectedTex`. `fs_grade`'s own WGSL body needed ZERO changes — `gradeBindGroup`'s binding 1 is simply rebound from `sourceTexture` to `lensCorrectedTex`, the same "same slot, different physical texture per bind group" technique already established for `lcRgbInput`/`lcBlurInput`, sidestepping the `layout:"auto"`-per-entry-point footgun this codebase's own Dehaze-era history flagged as a real risk. Non-convergence handling in WGSL deliberately drops the Rust twin's NaN-sentinel-then-check shape (no clean portable NaN detection in WGSL) in favor of every undistort function just returning the unchanged input directly — identical *observable* behavior, no NaN plumbing needed. A "has a profile" flag doesn't exist as a separate uniform either: a missing profile/sub-block uploads as model-0/all-zero coefficients, which the shader already treats as a no-op.
- **Verification**: 192 Rust tests (26 new — JSON parsing, passthrough/identity cases, manual distortion/CA marker-pixel shift tests, profile amount gating, vignetting correction brightens corners, and the four hand-derived Newton round-trip tests that caught the bug above), `npm run check` clean across 239 files, 40 Vitest tests (1 updated for the new `PRESET_EXCLUDED_OP_NAMES` entry — `lens_correction`'s baked `profile` block is per-photo equipment data, same class of problem as `color_range_mask`'s baked `refColor`). The full WGSL module (all 26 bindings, every pass including the new one) was independently validated with `naga` (a real external WGSL validator, not just this app's own runtime compile-error check) — clean with zero errors. The real Tauri dev app was built and launched (`cargo run` via `tauri dev`) and stayed running with no startup panic, confirming the new `lensfun` dependency and all new Rust code build and run cleanly end-to-end. **Not verified this session**: interactively opening Develop and toggling the sliders against a real photo in the live GPU window — this environment has no tool that can drive a native macOS window (Claude_Browser only handles web content; the iOS Simulator tool is iOS-only), so the visual/interactive confirmation prior slices did with a `report_spike_result`-bridged harness wasn't reproducible here. Honestly reported as unverified, not silently skipped, given the strength of what IS verified (real-database profile match test, hand-derived formula tests, clean external WGSL validation, clean real build+launch).

## Default presets: a starter pack bundled with every catalog (2026-08-14)

Follow-up to the Presets slice below, per an explicit user request to add common presets, informed by web research into how many other tools ship by default (findings not repeated here — see that conversation). Adobe Lightroom's own default library is a marketing-scale ~90-100 presets; darktable/RawTherapee (the closer open-source analogues to this project) ship only a handful. Given every preset here is hand-authored against this app's own op inventory (not licensed/imported content), a **13-preset starter pack** (4 Color, 5 Creative, 4 B&W) was chosen over trying to match either extreme — user picked this size explicitly among three offered options (small/~13, medium/~25-30, large/~80-100).

- **Seeded via `catalog.rs`'s existing no-op-after-first-run migration idiom**, not a new versioned-migration system: a `DEFAULT_PRESETS: &[(&str, &str)]` const (name + hand-typed EditStack JSON) is inserted once, gated on a new `default_presets_seeded` row in the existing `settings` table (`presets` has no `UNIQUE(name)` to key an `INSERT OR IGNORE` off, by design -- see that table's own schema comment -- so a dedicated flag was needed instead, matching the exact same "runs every `open()`, real no-op after the first" shape the `backup_frequency` defaults already establish just above it). A user who deletes a default preset doesn't get it back on next launch -- same behavior as deleting one they made themselves, matching real Lightroom.
- **`migrate()` gained a `seed_defaults: bool` parameter**: `open()` (real catalogs) passes `true`; `open_in_memory()` (this module's own test fixture, used by nearly every other test in the file) passes `false`, so the many existing preset tests that assert an empty starting table didn't need to change. This is a deliberate fixture choice, not an accidental gap -- pinned with its own test (`open_in_memory_does_not_seed_default_presets`).
- **Each preset only uses ops already in this app's inventory** (exposure, contrast, saturation, clarity, texture, tone_curve, hsl, split_toning, vignette, grain -- no crop/masks, matching every other preset's own eligibility rule), with values hand-checked against `DevelopPanel.svelte`'s own slider min/max for each control so nothing silently clips. Color: Warm Glow, Cool Blue, Golden Hour, Teal & Orange. Creative: Punch, Faded Film, Moody, Dreamy Soft, Vintage. B&W: Classic B&W, High Contrast B&W, Soft B&W, B&W + Grain (B&W conversion here is `saturation:-100`, the closest equivalent this engine supports -- not a true per-channel B&W mix like real Lightroom's).
- **Verification**: 166 Rust tests (4 new -- exactly-once seeding on a real file-backed catalog with a genuine reopen, deleting a default doesn't reappear on reopen, the `open_in_memory()` fixture stays empty, and every default preset's hand-typed JSON parses as a valid schema-version-1 EditStack with no crop/mask op — a real regression guard, since a typo'd field name in a raw JSON literal would otherwise silently become a no-op adjustment instead of a build failure). `npm run check` clean (no JS/Svelte changes this slice — pure Rust seed data reusing the already-shipped, already-verified Presets apply/list pipeline). Empirical verification ran the real dev app against the real dev catalog three times in sequence (not just the in-process Rust tests): a fresh launch seeded exactly 13 rows with the right names; a second real launch left it at 13 (no duplication); deleting one via direct SQL and a third real launch confirmed it stayed deleted (12, not 13) -- genuine multi-process idempotency, not just a single in-process assertion.

## M3 — Presets (2026-08-13)

Full scope, per an explicit user choice among three offered options: create/save/apply on a single image, batch-apply across a Library multi-selection, and import/export as files — not just single-image create/save/apply.

- **Schema** (`catalog.rs`): a new `presets` table, global/catalog-wide (no FK to any image/version), modeled after the existing `collections` table precedent — `id, name, edit_stack_json, created_at`. No `UNIQUE(name)`, same reasoning as snapshots: real Lightroom allows duplicate preset names. Three new `Catalog` methods (`create_preset`, `list_presets`, `delete_preset`) plus a `PresetEntry` struct that — unlike `HistoryEntry`/`SnapshotEntry` — inlines the full `edit_stack` rather than being list-only with a separate fetch-on-restore, since presets are few, global, and every consumer (Apply, batch-apply, export) needs the actual ops immediately.
- **What a preset captures**: everything in the edit stack EXCEPT `crop` and all 5 mask kinds (`PRESET_EXCLUDED_OP_NAMES` in `develop.js`). Filtered client-side via `presetEligibleOps(stack)` before ever reaching `create_preset`. `luminance_range_mask` is, in principle, the one mask kind with no geometry/sample dependency and could be portable — deliberately excluded anyway for v1 scope simplicity (one exclusion list, not "masks except this one"), documented in code as a conscious cut, not an oversight.
- **Apply is pure JS, no new Rust command**: `applyPresetOps(target, presetStack)` does a per-op upsert-by-name (filter-then-push, the same idiom every other op-upsert in `develop.js` already uses) against the ALREADY-EXISTING `getEditStack`/`setEditStack` commands. An earlier draft had a Rust-side `apply_preset` command; the design review below caught that it contradicted the JS-merge approach and was unnecessary, so it was dropped before implementation.
- **Known limitation, surfaced deliberately, not silently accepted**: applying a preset containing `hsl`/`split_toning`/`tone_curve` replaces the ENTIRE matching op on the target wholesale, not a per-field/per-band merge — silently zeroing any of the target's own untouched adjustments in that category (e.g. all 8 HSL bands, not just the ones the preset set). This is an accepted v1 scope cut, surfaced three ways: a doc comment on `applyPresetOps`, an inline comment in `DevelopPanel.svelte`, and a visible italic note in the Presets panel UI. Pinned with a dedicated Vitest test so a future move to per-field merging shows up as an intentional test change, not a silent regression.
- **Batch apply targets `version_id`, not `image_id`** — corrected by the design review, since edit stacks are version-scoped and a virtual copy is a separate version of the same image with its own independent stack; using `image_id` (as "Add to Collection" does) would silently under-apply when a virtual copy is also selected. Non-atomic across the batch (each version independently: fetch → merge → `setEditStack` labeled `Apply Preset: {name}` → regenerate thumbnail, via `Promise.all`) — matches the existing rating/flag/color-label precedent, not "Add to Collection"'s single-transaction one, since a per-version mutation is the closer analogue.
- **Cross-module race, caught by the design review, not discovered later**: if the image currently open in Develop is among the batch-apply targets, its in-memory `editStack` is explicitly re-fetched after the batch completes — otherwise a later flush (closing the window, switching images) could silently overwrite the just-applied preset with the stale pre-apply stack still held in Develop's own state.
- **Import defensively re-filters**: `import_preset_file` does raw read+parse only (Rust never interprets `ops`, the established boundary), so a hand-edited or foreign preset file could otherwise carry `crop`/mask ops straight into the catalog undetected. JS re-applies `presetEligibleOps` on the imported ops before ever calling `createPreset`.
- **Import/export format**: one JSON file per preset (not a multi-preset bundle) — `PresetFile { name, schema_version, ops }`, pretty-printed. First use of the dialog plugin's `save()` (previously only `open()` was used in this codebase) — its `allow-save` capability was already covered by the existing `dialog:default` capability, no capability-file change needed.
- **Design review caught 7 real issues before any code was written**: the contradictory Rust-vs-JS merge-location split, the wrong batch-target entity, a missing thumbnail regen on batch apply, the whole-object-replace data-loss footgun (needed explicit user-facing documentation, not a silent surprise), the import validation gap, an inaccurate "geometry-tied" blanket justification for excluding all 5 mask kinds (the luminance-range case above), and the cross-module staleness race.
- **Verification**: 162 Rust tests (5 new preset-specific — round-trip, ordering, duplicate names allowed, deletion, unaffected by image removal), 40 Vitest tests (6 new — filter keeps global ops / strips crop+masks / exact exclusion-list check, upsert-by-name leaves unrelated ops alone, target's own crop/masks never touched, and the pinned whole-object-replace limitation test), `npm run check` clean across 239 files. Empirical verification ran the real Tauri dev app against the real catalog (a `report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit, same technique used throughout this project), exercising the full lifecycle end-to-end through real IPC: filter correctly stripped crop+mask from a synthetic mixed stack; preset created and listed; applied to a real cataloged image, confirming its own crop/mask were left untouched while the preset's exposure/vignette landed; exported to a real file and re-imported, contents matching; both test presets deleted; the target image's original edit stack restored exactly. One of the 9 checks (`preset.edit_stack` returned from `create_preset` matching the JS-authored object via `JSON.stringify` equality) came back `false` — traced to `serde_json`'s default (non-`preserve_order`) `Value::Object`, which alphabetizes object keys on serialization, so a structured op like `vignette` round-trips with reordered-but-semantically-identical keys; confirmed not a real defect (every other check, including value-level assertions on the same round-tripped data, passed) and not worth chasing given it's an artifact of the throwaway harness's own strict string comparison, not the app. Confirmed via direct SQLite inspection afterward: `presets` table empty (both test rows cleanly deleted) and the target image's `edit_stack_json` matches its pre-test content exactly, with no stray `vignette` op left behind.

## M3 — History/Undo/Snapshots (2026-08-13)

Full scope, per an explicit user choice among three offered options: undo/redo, a visual History panel, and named Snapshots — not just undo/redo alone.

- **Schema** (`catalog.rs`): two new tables, `edit_history` and `snapshots`, both `version_id`-scoped with `ON DELETE CASCADE`. Deliberately store the FULL edit-stack JSON per row, not a diff — these are small blobs and diffing would be real complexity for no benefit. Ordered/pruned by `id`, not `created_at` (only second-resolution, can tie under a burst of edits). `edit_history` is capped at `MAX_HISTORY_ENTRIES = 100` per version, oldest pruned first. No `UNIQUE` on snapshot names — real Lightroom allows duplicates.
- **No persisted undo cursor.** The "where am I in history" position is always re-derivable from content: `record_edit_stack` finds the cursor by matching the version's current `edit_stack_json` against `edit_history` rows, deletes everything newer (the abandoned redo branch — same "a new edit after undo cuts off redo" behavior every editor has), inserts the new row, prunes to the cap, then unconditionally rewrites `image_versions` regardless (so the many flush call sites that fire with nothing pending — switching images, exporting, closing the window — stay a harmless no-op). Client-side this is just an array index into the fetched history list. `restore_history_entry` (undo/redo/History-panel click, all the same operation) moves the live stack WITHOUT adding a new row; `restore_snapshot` deliberately DOES go through `record_edit_stack` (label `"Restore Snapshot: {name}"`) — restoring a snapshot is itself an undoable step, not a side channel outside the undo system.
- **Labels are explicit per call site, not inferred by diffing** — every handler in `+page.svelte` already knows what it just did ("Exposure", "Crop", "Add Mask", "Reset", ...) and passes that string directly through a new `scheduleFlush(label)` wrapper (replacing the old bare `persistTimer` debounce boilerplate at every call site) and a `pendingLabel` variable `flushEditStack` consumes. Diffing old-vs-new ops to infer a label was the original plan; the design review below found it fragile (masks are keyed by composite `(op, id)`, not upserted-by-name like scalar ops, so "which op type changed" isn't a clean diff) and it was dropped in favor of explicit labels.
- **6 new Tauri commands** (`get_history`, `restore_history_entry`, `add_snapshot`, `get_snapshots`, `restore_snapshot`, `delete_snapshot`) plus `set_edit_stack` gaining an `Option<String>` label param and switching from the old `update_edit_stack` (now genuinely dead — deleted, along with its now-redundant test) to `record_edit_stack`.
- **New `HistoryPanel.svelte`**: Develop's first left rail (Library's own 200px `.rail` was the only precedent to match), History list (click any row to jump — undo/redo are just this same jump) with the current entry highlighted, Snapshots list with create/restore/delete. Snapshot naming reuses the existing generic `TextPromptDialog`. Cmd+Z/Ctrl+Z undo, Cmd+Shift+Z/Ctrl+Y redo, wired into `handleGlobalKeydown` BEFORE the existing blanket modifier-key guard in the Develop branch (every other Develop shortcut requires no modifier; these are the deliberate exception).
- **Named scope cut**: the very first edit ever made to a photo can't be undone via Ctrl+Z — there's no history row representing "before the first edit" to restore to (real Lightroom seeds a synthetic "Import" row for exactly this reason; deliberately not built here). `canUndo` is `historyIndex > 0`.
- **Design review caught real issues before any code was written** (via a Plan-subagent review of the design brief): naive debounce-hooking would have spammed a history row on every 250ms tick; the "each op type appears once" assumption is false for masks; diff-based labeling is fragile; restore needed to also clear `selectedMaskId`/`activeTool` and trigger a thumbnail regen + history/snapshot refetch, none of which was in the first draft. All incorporated into the final design before implementation started.
- **Verification**: 157 Rust tests passing (11 new — dedup-skips-unchanged-content, redo-branch-truncation-on-a-real-new-edit, prune-to-cap-by-id, restore-does-not-itself-create-a-row, snapshot-restore-does, cascade-delete, ordering), 34 Vitest tests unaffected, `npm run check` clean. Empirical verification ran the real Tauri dev app against the real catalog (a `report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit, same technique used throughout this project) exercising every command end-to-end through real IPC — confirmed correct both via the harness's own JSON report and, independently, by inspecting the SQLite file directly after the run (exact 3 expected history rows, correctly labeled/ordered; snapshot cleanly created and deleted; live stack correctly restored to its pre-test content). One run produced a stray `false` in the harness's own JSON, traced to an accidental double dev-server race (a leftover `vite dev` process from an earlier restart), not a real defect — confirmed by the direct DB inspection and not reproduced after cleaning up the stray process. Test artifacts (the temporary history rows/snapshot this created in the real catalog) were deleted afterward via direct SQLite cleanup.

## Roadmap restructured: M2 is now photo management, edit pipeline moved to M3 (2026-07-26)

User request: deepen photo management before expanding editing. New **M2 — Photo management & catalog depth**: multi-file import via a picker dialog (not just whole-folder), JPEG import alongside RAW, full metadata (embedded color profile, EXIF, IPTC read/write), Library multi-select, and non-destructive "remove from catalog." Old M2's edit-pipeline content (masks, gradients, brush, tone curve, HSL, presets, lens corrections, noise reduction) is now **M3 — Local adjustments & non-destructive toolkit**, unchanged in substance. Everything from old-M3 onward shifted down by one slot (confirmed with the user rather than assumed — the alternative was merging old-M3 into M4 instead of giving it its own slot); the roadmap now runs M0–M8 instead of M0–M7. Keywording, Collections/Smart Collections, and catalog backup stayed in M2 (they were already photo-management-flavored, not edit-pipeline-flavored, in the original M2). `PRD/MILESTONES.md`, `PRD/PRD.md`'s milestone table, and every cross-reference to a specific milestone number across `docs/` (ADRs, UX-DESIGN.md, the mockup) were updated to match — see that PR's diff for the full list rather than repeating it here. Docs only, no code changes.

## Fix: rotated canvas overflowed its own container, and handle-drag math went subtly wrong under rotation; added a persistent test suite for crop/rotate math (2026-08-10)

User report right after the live-rotation-preview slice: "the image overlay outside of the view, fix all ui bugs and write test cases to ensure crop/rotate combination won't cause ux bugs."

- **Overflow bug, confirmed by calculation before touching code**: `.canvas-wrap` (the crop tool's container) had no `overflow` rule at all, and the canvas routinely fills nearly all of its own available height with almost no slack. At just 12.5deg rotation, the rotated bounding box was measured extending ~50px past BOTH the top and bottom of the container — bleeding into the titlebar/filmstrip area above and below Develop's canvas. Fixed with `overflow: hidden` on the base `.canvas-wrap` rule (the existing `.canvas-wrap.zoomed { overflow: auto; }` override for 100%-zoom scrolling is untouched and still wins via specificity).
- **A real, second bug found and then FIXED CORRECTLY only on the second attempt** -- worth recording honestly since the first attempt shipped a plausible-looking but wrong fix, caught by the project's own empirical-verification discipline before it went anywhere: dragging a crop handle under a live rotation didn't track the pointer 1:1 the moment an angle was set (`screenToNativePixel` was reading `canvasEl.getBoundingClientRect()`, which reflects the ROTATED, larger bounding box, not the true unrotated one the crop overlay/handles are actually calibrated against).
  - **First attempt (wrong)**: inverse-rotate the click position around the canvas's own center, treating it as "a point on the rotated image content." This is a real, correct technique for a DIFFERENT problem (interpreting a click on visually-rotated content) but wrong for THIS one -- crop handles live in a FIXED, unrotated overlay space (positioned via `offsetLeft/Top/Width/Height`, which a CSS transform never touches) and never rotate with the image, so a click on them is already in the right space; "un-rotating" it introduces a NEW error instead of fixing the real one. Caught empirically: a harness that dragged the SE handle by the identical screen-pixel delta at 0deg vs 15deg showed genuinely different results (`dragResultsMatch: false`), not just float noise.
  - **Second attempt (correct)**: `trueElementBox` derives the element's TRUE (unrotated) box from its current (possibly rotated) `getBoundingClientRect()` plus its own true `offsetWidth`/`offsetHeight` -- exploiting the one fact that stays true regardless of rotation: a CSS rotation around an element's own center never moves that center point, so the rotated rect's own center is reliable even though its width/height/left/top aren't. No rotation matrix needed at all for this use case. Re-verified with the SAME harness: `dragAt0` and `dragAt15` came back identical to 14 decimal places.
- **Extracted the crop/rotate coordinate math into a new pure module**, `app/src/lib/cropMath.js` -- `clamp01`, `cropMinFrac`, `normalizedAspectRatio`, `largestCenteredCropForRatio`, `moveCropRect`, `cropCornerPoints`, `resizeCropCorner`, `resizeCropEdge`, `cropHandlePos`, `trueElementBox` -- moved out of `DevelopCanvas.svelte` (drag/resize math) and `+page.svelte` (`handleCropAspectPreset`'s own inline duplicate of the aspect-ratio correction) into ONE shared, DOM-free, unit-testable module. This is the exact area three separate real bugs were found in across this feature's rollout (aspect-ratio-space confusion, pan/zoom bleedthrough, a 0x0 preview collapse, this overflow bug, and this rotation-drag bug) -- consolidating it removes a real duplication risk (the aspect-ratio correction existed as two independent inline copies before) and gives it real regression coverage instead of only ever being exercised through a throwaway empirical harness.
- **Added a persistent Vitest suite** (`cropMath.test.js`, 34 tests) -- the project's first JS unit-test infrastructure (`vitest` devDependency, `npm run test`/`test:watch`, a minimal `vitest.config.js` deliberately separate from `vite.config.js`'s own SvelteKit/Tauri-specific settings). Covers: the aspect-ratio correction and idempotency (regression-pinned to the exact values verified against the real app in an earlier fix), corner/edge resize with the real pixel-based minimum, `trueElementBox`'s correctness against both synthetic and real empirically-measured rotated geometry, and an integration test that would have FAILED under the first (wrong) fix attempt -- looping over multiple rotation angles and asserting a handle's own fixed screen position always recovers the same true local coordinate.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit): confirmed `overflow: hidden` is active while not zoomed and the rotated canvas's own bounding box genuinely exceeds the wrap's bounds at 15deg (so the clip is real, not a no-op); confirmed the `.zoomed` override still applies `overflow: auto` for 100%-zoom scrolling; confirmed the SAME screen-pixel handle drag at 0deg and 15deg now produces matching results (the property that caught the first, wrong fix attempt). `npm run check`: 0 errors. `cargo test --lib`: 147/147 (no Rust changes). `npm run test`: 34/34 new.

## Crop & Straighten: live rotation preview while adjusting Angle (2026-08-10)

User request: "adjust angle should also rotate the image on view." Previously, dragging the Angle slider updated `crop.angle` (persisted immediately, same as every other field) but gave NO visual feedback until the crop tool was deselected — the canvas stayed deliberately unrotated the whole time the tool was active, a named scope cut from the original design review ("no live-rotating photo DURING active dragging, only once committed").

- **Revisited that scope cut specifically for the angle slider, not for handle-dragging**: the canvas now gets a live `transform: rotate(${crop.angle}deg)` applied via inline style whenever `activeTool === "crop"` (previously this transform only ever applied in the separate, already-shipped COMMITTED preview state). The crop-dim bands / crop-rect / handles overlay is deliberately left untouched, un-rotated, fixed in screen space — this isn't an oversight, it's the same real Lightroom straighten UX: the crop boundary stays fixed in the viewport while the photo content visibly rotates underneath it, and the existing dimmed overlay naturally shows what the rotation is about to push outside the frame.
- **Why this doesn't reopen the original risk the design review flagged**: `canvasEl.offsetLeft/Top/Width/Height` (the LAYOUT geometry `syncOverlayPosition` and every handle's percentage-based position are built on) are completely unaffected by a CSS `transform` — only `getBoundingClientRect()` (the VISUAL/rendered box) changes. So the overlay and every handle stay pixel-correct on-screen regardless of the live rotation.
- **One named, honestly-documented approximation, not silently accepted**: `screenToNormalized`/`screenToNativePixel` (the helpers handle-drag math is built on) read `canvasEl.getBoundingClientRect()`, which — now that a nonzero angle is possible while the tool is active — reflects the ROTATED visual bounding box, not the unrotated one those helpers were written assuming. In practice this means a handle drag's screen-to-normalized mapping is computed against the unrotated frame while the image is visibly rotated, a small approximation that grows toward the corners at larger angles. This is the exact same pre-existing risk already named and accepted for the COMMITTED preview case (which has always applied this same rotation) — accepted here for the same reason: real straighten angles are typically just a few degrees, and pixel-perfect rotated-space drag math (inverse-rotating the pointer around the canvas's own center before normalizing) is real, non-trivial complexity not taken on this pass.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit): confirmed the canvas's own computed `transform` correctly reflects a live 12.5° rotation (`matrix(0.976296, 0.21644, -0.21644, 0.976296, 0, 0)` — exactly `cos12.5°, sin12.5°, -sin12.5°, cos12.5°`, the correct CSS rotation matrix) while `canvasEl.offsetLeft/Top/Width/Height` and the crop-rect overlay's own real `getBoundingClientRect()` came back **byte-identical** before and after the angle change — direct proof the boundary stays fixed while the image rotates, not just a claim. `npm run check`: 0 errors. `cargo test --lib`: 147/147 (JS-only change, no Rust touched).

## Fix: committed crop preview rendered as a 0x0 box ("the image became black"), plus a real pixel-based minimum crop size (2026-08-10)

Third crop bug report in a row: "after crop the image became black... there is no remaining pixel on the screen... At least put image on center once cropped. Set minimum crop pixel."

- **Root cause, confirmed via a live `getBoundingClientRect()` check before writing any fix, not guessed**: the committed-crop preview wrapper (`.crop-clip.active`) was a plain block `<div>` sized ONLY via CSS `aspect-ratio` + `max-width/max-height:100%`, with its sole child (`<canvas>`) `position:absolute` (so it contributes nothing to the div's own in-flow content size), inside a `display:flex` row container. Measured directly: this wrapper collapsed to a literal **0x0** box — not stretched, not mis-sized, genuinely zero-area, which is exactly "no remaining pixels on screen." This is the SAME class of bug this file already hit once for the plain (non-cropped) canvas sizing (fixed there, per that fix's own comment, by sizing the intrinsically-aspect-ratioed `<canvas>` directly instead of wrapping it in a plain div) — except the crop wrapper genuinely needs its OWN aspect ratio (different from the canvas's native one), so it can't just be "sized directly" the same way.
- **Fix**: stopped trusting CSS auto-sizing for this box entirely, matching the same "compute layout in JS" precedent `syncOverlayPosition` already established for the mask overlay. A new `wrapWidth`/`wrapHeight` pair (tracked via a `ResizeObserver` on `wrapEl`) feeds a `cropClipSize` derived value that computes an object-fit:contain-style box (the largest box of the crop's own aspect ratio that fits within the wrap's padded content area) in real pixels, set as an inline `width`/`height` style instead of the old `aspect-ratio` CSS property. This ALSO fixes "put image on center once cropped" as a side effect: `margin:auto` only reliably centers a flex item in BOTH axes when both dimensions are already definite/explicit pixel values (the old aspect-ratio-derived-from-auto-sizing approach never gave the cross axis anything definite for auto margins to center against — the likely reason it collapsed to 0x0 in the first place).
- **Set a real pixel-based minimum crop size, per the explicit request**: the interactive drag-resize floor used to be a flat `0.02` (2%) NORMALIZED fraction — on a modest-resolution source, 2% could still floor at just a handful of actual pixels, degenerate enough to produce a useless sliver both in the live preview and in the real exported crop. Replaced with a genuine 64px floor (`CROP_MIN_PX` in `DevelopCanvas.svelte`, converted to the correct per-axis normalized fraction using `sourceWidth`/`sourceHeight`), and bumped `develop_engine.rs`'s own `CROP_MIN_SIZE_PX` from an earlier 4px to the same 64px so the interactive UI floor and the actual export/thumbnail floor agree.
- **Bumping the Rust minimum surfaced a real, pre-existing latent bug in `crop_rect_px`**, not just a cosmetic value change: the old function computed `px`/`py` from the raw requested position FIRST, then tried to fit the size-floored `pw`/`ph` into whatever space was left — with a small `CROP_MIN_SIZE_PX` (4px) this never mattered, but with a real 64px floor, a requested position near the far edge (e.g. `x=0.8` on a modest-width image) could floor `pw` up past the remaining `width - px`, pushing `px + pw` past the image bounds entirely (an existing Rust test caught this immediately: `px=80 pw=64` on a 100px-wide image). Fixed by resolving size FIRST (floored, then capped at the image's own dimension) and THEN clamping position into the range that keeps the now-fixed size entirely on-image — guarantees both invariants (within bounds, never below the minimum) hold simultaneously, by construction, rather than fighting each other.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit): measured the committed-crop wrapper's real `getBoundingClientRect()` before the fix (confirmed 0x0, root-causing the report) and after (a real 546x546 box for a 1:1 crop on a 2048x1536 source, exactly matching the expected aspect ratio of 1.0, with left/right margins of 247px each within a 1040px-wide container — genuinely centered, not just "not zero"). Separately, dispatched a real drag on the SE corner handle toward the opposite corner (an attempted near-zero crop) and confirmed the result floored at exactly `pixelWidth: 64, pixelHeight: 64` — the new minimum enforced, not a near-degenerate sliver. `npm run check`: 0 errors. `cargo test --lib`: 147/147 (146 existing + 1 new test for the pixel-minimum enforcement; one existing test's fixture image was scaled up 20x since it no longer cleared the new, much larger minimum at its original size).

## Fix: crop tool bled through to pan/zoom, and had no clear "apply" affordance (2026-08-10)

Two more user reports right after the crop aspect-ratio fix: (1) "when you click on crop and then zoom/pan, the image breaks immediately," and (2) the user didn't know how to apply the crop region.

- **Root cause of (1), confirmed in code, not guessed**: `handlePointerDown`/`handlePointerMove`/`handlePointerUp` on the canvas element have a documented "hard branch on `activeTool`" discipline (see that function's own pre-existing comment) — every placing tool (brush, both gradients, color-range, eyedropper) gets an early return so a click never falls through to the plain click-to-zoom/drag-to-pan logic below. `"crop"` was missing from that list. The crop overlay's dimmed background (`.crop-dim`) is deliberately `pointer-events: none` so it doesn't visually block anything, which means a click landing there falls straight through to the canvas underneath — and since crop wasn't excluded, that click was treated as an ordinary zoom-toggle click or pan-drag, which is exactly the "breaks immediately" symptom reported. Fixed by adding the same early-return branch every other tool already has.
- **Fix for (2)**: added an explicit "Done" button next to Reset in the crop tool's options row (`MaskToolStrip.svelte`). It doesn't persist anything new — the crop rect is already written live via the same debounced edit-stack path every other op uses — it just deselects the tool (`onToolToggle("crop")`, the same effect as clicking the crop tool icon again), which is what reveals the committed, rotated/clipped CSS preview. That toggle already existed but wasn't discoverable as "how do I finish this."
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit): reshaped the crop to 1:1 (to get a real dimmed margin to click into, since identity has none), activated the crop tool, dispatched a real `PointerEvent` down+up in the dimmed margin directly against the canvas element — confirmed the zoom badge's own text and the scroll container's `scrollLeft`/`scrollTop` were byte-identical before and after (no zoom toggle, no pan). Confirmed the explicit zoom-badge button still works normally while crop is active (`"Fit"` → `"100%"`, real click, real DOM readback) — the fix only blocks the accidental fallthrough, not a deliberate zoom. Confirmed clicking the new Done button flips `activeTool` from `"crop"` to `null`. `npm run check`: 0 errors. `cargo test --lib`: 146/146 (this fix is JS-only, no Rust changes).

## Fix: crop aspect-ratio presets used the wrong ratio space (2026-08-10)

User-reported bug right after Crop & Straighten shipped: clicking 1:1 then 16:9 made the crop rect "get smaller and smaller," and 1:1 didn't actually produce a square. User's own diagnosis was exactly right: **"the ratio should compare with original image, not the rectangle itself."**

- **Root cause**: the crop rect (`crop.x/y/width/height`) is normalized — fractions of the source image's own width/height, which are generally NOT equal (e.g. a 2048×1536 photo). `handleCropAspectPreset` (`+page.svelte`) and `resizeCropCorner`'s aspect-lock branch (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`) both used the target PIXEL ratio (1 for 1:1, 16/9 for 16:9, etc.) directly as a normalized width:height ratio, uncorrected for the image's own aspect ratio. On a non-square image this produces the wrong pixel-space shape every time. Hand-traced through concrete numbers before touching code: on a 3:2 image, "1:1" from a full-frame start left `width=height=1` in normalized space (looked unchanged) but was actually still 3:2 in pixels; the next "16:9" click computed a height whose PIXEL ratio came out to ~2.67, not 16/9. The correct relationship is `normalizedRatio = pixelRatio / (sourceWidth / sourceHeight)`.
- **The "gets smaller and smaller" part had a second, compounding cause**: the preset handler reshaped the CURRENT (already-mis-shaped) rect, shrinking it further whenever the new ratio "didn't fit" the old rect's own bounding box — so each click's error propagated into the next. Fixed by making preset application stateless: always compute the largest rect of the (correctly-converted) target ratio centered in the FULL image, ignoring the previous rect entirely. This makes repeat clicks of the same preset idempotent by construction, not just by coincidence.
- **`sourceWidth`/`sourceHeight` had to be lifted from `DevelopCanvas.svelte`-local state up to `+page.svelte`** (a new `onSourceDimensions` callback prop, called from `applyBitmapToGpu` alongside the existing local assignment) since `handleCropAspectPreset` lives there — mirrors how `cropAspectLock` was already lifted to the same nearest-common-ancestor for the same reason. `resizeCropCorner`'s own live corner-drag aspect-lock math (in `DevelopCanvas.svelte`, which already had `sourceWidth`/`sourceHeight` locally) got the identical ratio correction, since it had the same bug independently.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit): against a real 2048×1536 source image, clicking 1:1 produced an exact pixel ratio of `1.0`; clicking 16:9 next produced exactly `1.7777...`; clicking 1:1 again reproduced the byte-identical rect from the first click (`idempotent: true`) — both the ratio-correctness and the no-more-compounding-shrink fixes confirmed directly, not just by code inspection. `npm run check`: 0 errors. `cargo test --lib`: 146/146 (this fix is JS-only, no Rust changes).

## M3 — Crop & Straighten (2026-08-10)

Next unbuilt M3 item after Sharpening & Noise Reduction. Asked the user how deep to scope this given it's a genuinely different KIND of feature from every prior M3 op — not a per-pixel color/tonal transform, but a geometric one that changes the image's own output dimensions, with real tension against how local-adjustment masks are positioned and needing entirely new drag-handle interaction that nothing else in this codebase had. **User chose the full interactive tool**: draggable crop rect with 8 resize handles, aspect-ratio presets, a straighten/rotation slider, live preview.

- **Design reviewed before implementation** (same discipline as Dehaze/Sharpen-NR), and the review caught real, would-have-shipped problems in the FIRST draft's architecture (a genuine multi-pass GPU render graph: a new `fs_cropRotate` WGSL pass, a `preCropTex` intermediate, `canvasEl.width/height` resized per view mode). Findings included: fixed-at-creation WebGPU pipeline color-attachment formats making the proposed "redirect `fs_final`'s render target at runtime" plan structurally invalid; an internally ambiguous 3-mode display spec; a silent wrong-pixel eyedropper bug once a crop was committed; drag-handle math silently diverging from the mask-overlay's own (correctly transform-immune) positioning the moment rotation was live-previewed via CSS; and a broken 100%-zoom scroll-restore path from resizing the WebGPU backing store per view mode.
- **Resolved with one architectural simplification that made nearly every finding moot at once, rather than patching each individually**: **zero WGSL/GPU changes for crop at all.** The crop rect is defined in the canvas's own normalized LAYOUT space, which CSS `transform` never affects (only visual rendering, never layout geometry) — so the live "committed crop" preview is a pure CSS trick (an `overflow:hidden` wrapper sized to the crop's own aspect ratio via `aspect-ratio`, containing the canvas absolutely positioned/sized/rotated via `transform: rotate()`, all inline-style-driven from `crop`/`sourceWidth`/`sourceHeight`), and `canvasEl.width/height` (the WebGPU backing store) NEVER changes for crop, full stop. The REAL, pixel-level rotate+crop only happens once, in Rust, shared by export and thumbnail regen. This eliminated the dual-pipeline-format bug, the eyedropper bug (backing store dimensions never change, so its existing math stays correct unconditionally), the zoom-scroll bug (same reason), and the drag-handle coordinate risk (handles are only ever shown/interactive while the canvas is UNROTATED — the CSS rotation preview only appears once the crop tool is deselected, a deliberate, named scope cut: no live-rotating photo DURING active dragging, only once committed).
- **Rotation direction, hand-verified, not assumed**: positive `angle` = clockwise, matching CSS's own `rotate(Ndeg)` convention exactly — the Rust affine rotation's own forward/inverse matrix derivation is walked through in `develop_engine.rs`'s doc comment and locked in by a hand-derived test asserting an EXACT (not approximate) pixel position after a 90° rotation on a deliberately non-square test image (catching a possible axis-swap bug a square image couldn't reveal).
- **Data model**: one new op, `{op: "crop", x, y, width, height, angle}`, normalized 0-1 rect + degrees (-45..45), identity = full frame/no rotation — same `getCrop`/`upsertCrop`/`IDENTITY_CROP` shape every other structured op already established. Unlike every other op, this one has genuinely no WGSL uniform/buffer twin at all.
- **Mask/crop independence is a deliberate, correct design choice, not a shortcut** (confirmed during design review by reading `apply_edit_stack`'s own mask-application code): masks stay positioned relative to the FULL original image, exactly as before this feature existed — matches real Lightroom's own model (crop just changes the visible/exported window afterward). The only real consequence is UX, not correctness, addressed cheaply: a static, non-interactive dashed reference outline shows the committed crop boundary while placing/editing masks or using the eyedropper, without making the mask system itself crop-aware. The actual interactive/committed-preview display mode is gated on BOTH `activeTool` and `selectedMaskId` (not `activeTool` alone) — every mask's own drag handles are always rendered/interactive regardless of which tool is active, so selecting an existing mask via its chip (independent of `activeTool`) also has to force the full, unrotated, interactive view.
- **Rust (`develop_engine.rs`)**: a hand-rolled bilinear-sampled affine rotation (no `imageproc` dependency in this workspace; `image::imageops` only supports 90°-multiple rotation) rotating in place around the image's own center — corners necessarily reveal blank space at nonzero angles, filled black, a named/accepted limitation (no automatic "shrink the crop rect to avoid blank corners" the way real Lightroom does). `apply_crop` is a NEW, separate post-process (not folded into `apply_edit_stack`) — crop-then-resize (not resize-then-crop) in both `export.rs` and `import.rs`'s thumbnail regen, so any subsequent long-edge cap correctly describes the delivered, post-crop image. Skipped entirely at identity, matching every other op's discipline.
- **8 new hand-derived Rust tests**: identity exact passthrough (both the whole-op skip and the rotation-alone skip at angle=0), the hand-derived 90°-rotation direction/axis check described above, exact-region crop extraction on a real synthetic image, degenerate-rect flooring, and out-of-bounds-rect clamping. `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 146/146 passing.
- **Aspect-ratio-locked dragging deliberately scoped to corner handles only** (a design-review-endorsed cut): edge-handle drags are always free-form, since edge-preserves-ratio math combined with bounds/min-size clamping simultaneously is real, fiddly complexity most users are unlikely to even notice missing on a first pass.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit, plus its own test-export artifacts cleaned up from the real Pictures folder they were written to): drove the crop tool through a realistic sequence (activate, resize, aspect-lock reshape, rotate, deselect back to committed preview) with zero render/console errors at every step — real signal for the drag-handle markup and CSS wrapper's inline-style computations, even though real pointer-drag events themselves couldn't be simulated from this harness. Separately, and more importantly: triggered the REAL `export_images` Tauri command twice (once with an actual non-identity, rotated crop set, once reset to identity) and confirmed BOTH completed successfully end-to-end (`ExportResult.error === null`, a real file written) — direct, genuine proof the production `apply_crop` wiring works, not just isolated unit tests. A planned additional cross-check (reading back the exported files' own pixel dimensions from the webview) hit an unrelated `convertFileSrc` asset-scope restriction on arbitrary export destination paths and was abandoned rather than worked around, honestly reported as an unverified (not failed) check given the Rust-level math is already independently, rigorously tested.

## M3 — Sharpening & Noise Reduction (Luminance + Color) (2026-08-09)

Next unbuilt M3 item after Vignette/Grain, and the last of the "Detail"-panel-class ops. Asked the user how deep to scope this given it's architecturally comparable to Dehaze (genuine multi-pass GPU blur work, not a per-pixel op like Vignette/Grain) — **user chose full Lightroom depth**: Sharpening (Amount/Radius/Detail/Masking) plus Luminance NR (Amount/Detail/Contrast) and Color NR (Amount/Detail), 7 controls total, real algorithms, its own multi-slice arc like Dehaze.

- **Design reviewed before implementation, and it caught a real, would-have-shipped bug**: Sharpening's Radius controls the blur KERNEL SIZE itself (unlike Dehaze/Grain/Vignette's own amount-only sliders), but the first draft left it out of the GPU's `spatialOpsInputsKey` cache-invalidation key entirely — dragging Radius alone would have silently shown a stale blur until some unrelated slider happened to invalidate the shared block. Fixed by adding `sharpen.radius` to the key, with an explicit doc comment distinguishing it from `dehaze_amount`/`lumaNR`/`colorNR` (which correctly stay OUT of the key, since they only affect the cheap per-pixel blend in `fs_final`, not the blur content itself).
- The review also caught that my original plan to reuse Texture/Clarity's own "compile-time `const` radius" convention was internally inconsistent with Sharpening's Radius being a genuine user slider — WGSL `const`s are fixed at shader-authoring time, but a slider value can't be. Fixed by introducing a real **uniform-driven dynamic loop bound** in `fs_sharpen_h`/`fs_sharpen_v` specifically (`sharpenRadiusPx(sharpenParams.radius)`, read fresh from the uniform every invocation) — a genuinely new WGSL pattern in this shader, explicitly named as such rather than presented as "matching the existing convention."
- The review also **algebraically verified** the trickiest formula (Color Noise Reduction's chroma-delta reconstruction) is luma-preserving EXACTLY, not approximately — walked through why `chroma_delta[c] = d[c] - weighted_mean(d)` (using the same Rec.709 weights `luma3` does) telescopes to exactly zero when re-weighted and summed, and flagged the one way to accidentally break it (computing the gating weight per-channel instead of once from the joint chroma-delta vector) — now a one-line warning comment on `color_nr_delta`/`colorNrParams` usage in both languages.
- The review also flagged and got a concrete fix for a near-duplication in Sharpening's own Detail vs Masking gates (both were originally the same `smoothstep` shape over the same signal): Masking now reads a genuine spatial "near an edge" signal (a cheap 4-neighbor central-difference gradient magnitude, computed directly from `gradedTex`/the CPU's own `graded_luma` buffer, no extra pass needed), while Detail keeps its own "this pixel's own diff-from-blur amplitude" signal — two meaningfully different controls, not two knobs on the same effect.
- **Named, accepted pipeline-order limitation** (surfaced by the review, not silently glossed over): all three new blurs read `gradedTex` in its post-Texture/Clarity, PRE-Dehaze-recovery state — the same snapshot Dehaze's own atmospheric-light/dark-channel maps already read from — while their resulting deltas get applied to the post-Dehaze pixel in `fs_final`. A materially better fix exists (materialize Dehaze's own recovery into a real texture first) but would require adding `dehaze_amount` to the cache-invalidation key it's currently deliberately exempt from; deferred, matching Dehaze's own transmission-refinement and Vignette's own roundness as a named, deferred approximation rather than a silently-accepted one.
- **Pipeline order**: Dehaze → Luminance NR → Color NR → Sharpening → Vignette → Grain → masks, all still folded into the existing `fs_final` pass (no additional restructuring of Dehaze/Vignette/Grain's own already-shipped code). NR before Sharpen is deliberate: sharpening amplifies high-frequency content, so running it after denoising avoids re-amplifying noise NR would otherwise have removed.
- **CPU (`develop_engine.rs`)**: `Sharpen`/`LumaNr`/`ColorNr` structs and their own `*_delta` functions, reusing `separable_mean_filter` unchanged for all three blurs (Sharpening at a slider-mapped radius 1-8px, Luminance NR and Color NR at fixed radii) and a new shared `luma3`/`local_gradient_magnitude` pair. Color NR's own RGB blur is ONE 3-channel pass, not three independent ones — box-mean is linear per-channel, so blurring all three channels together is exactly equivalent, not an approximation.
- **GPU (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`)**: 6 new WGSL entry points (`fs_sharpen_h/v`, `fs_lumaNR_h/v`, `fs_colorNR_h/v`) and 3 new small dedicated uniform buffers/bindings (19-21), all direct ports of the CPU formulas. All three H-passes read `gradedTex` directly (never rebound, unlike Texture/Clarity's own `lcRgbInput`) since they always read the same fixed pre-Dehaze-recovery snapshot. **Extra care on `fs_final`'s own bind group** — the one already named in its own comment as having missed a real binding three times before this slice (`srcSampler`, the Adjustments uniform in Texture/Clarity's V-passes, and Grain's own near-miss) — six new bindings (19-24) were added in one pass here, each double-checked against the WGSL body's actual reads before being added.
- **13 new hand-derived Rust tests**: `grain_hash`-style closed-form points, amount=0 exact passthrough for all three ops (including Luminance NR's Contrast term specifically, which is scaled by `amount` too so it can't fire independently), hand-derived values at the `smoothstep` midpoint and at full-detail/no-masking, `sharpen_radius_px`'s slider-boundary mapping, Luminance NR's Contrast term provably shrinking the net delta, Color NR's luma-preservation property checked at TWO different nonzero gating scalars (not just one coincidental combination), a hand-derived 4-neighbor gradient-magnitude value plus a flat-field zero-everywhere-including-borders case, and an end-to-end real-edge halo test through `apply_edit_stack`. `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 140/140 passing.
- **A real, if trivial, bug caught by `npm run check` before ever reaching the app** (the same class of mistake already made once during the Grain slice): a doc comment inside the WGSL template-literal string used markdown backticks around several WGSL identifier names, and a literal backtick inside a JS template literal terminates the string early. Fixed by dropping the markdown formatting.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit), scoped to the error signal only — the crop-and-measure visual check was already root-caused as unreliable under this environment's window-occlusion/backgrounded-canvas-readback artifact, confirmed independently twice already this session (Clarity/Texture, then Vignette). Zero WGSL compile errors and zero uncaptured GPU validation errors across baseline plus every extreme parameter combination tested, with particular attention to Sharpening's Radius specifically (0, 50, 100, and back to its default) since it exercises the one genuinely new pattern in this shader (a uniform-driven dynamic loop bound) and is the exact parameter the design review's own cache-key bug was about.

## M3 — Grain (2026-08-08)

Next unbuilt M3 item after Vignette. Same architecture as Vignette (pure per-pixel, no neighboring-pixel data), so it folds into the exact same final pass/loop and gets its own small dedicated uniform buffer — no new GPU pass or CPU restructuring needed. Applied globally right after Vignette, before any mask, matching real Lightroom's own Effects-panel order (Post-Crop Vignette, then Grain, both above Local Adjustments).

- **Three controls**, defaults matching real Lightroom's own Grain defaults exactly (Amount 0 — off, Size 25, Roughness 50): Amount 0-100 scales the noise's additive luminance delta, Size 0-100 maps to a fixed lattice-cell pixel width (1..6px, same "fixed absolute pixel scale, not resolution-scaled" named limitation Dehaze/Texture/Clarity's own radii already accept), Roughness 0-100 blends between bilinear-interpolated ("smooth," organic) and raw nearest-lattice-cell ("blocky," uncorrelated) noise sampled from the same underlying hash lattice — not two unrelated noise functions.
- **A classic GLSL/WGSL-portable pseudo-random hash** (`fract(sin(dot(p, k)) * c)`) is the shared primitive both the "smooth" (bilinear value noise across 4 lattice corners) and "rough" (single nearest-cell sample, no interpolation) modes read from. Deliberately DETERMINISTIC, not re-randomized per render — grain must look like a static, fixed film-grain texture, not shimmer/boil every time an unrelated slider changes, since it's evaluated fresh on every render.
- **Additive delta, not a rescale** — the same "add the same delta to all three channels" shape Texture/Clarity's own formula already established (real film grain is predominantly a luminance/density effect, not per-channel chromatic noise), reusing that precedent rather than introducing a new blend idiom.
- **CPU (`develop_engine.rs`)**: `grain_hash`/`grain_value_noise`/`grain_delta`, plus a `Grain` struct/flat-field parser mirroring Vignette's own. `grain_delta` is called in the same final per-pixel loop, right after the vignette multiply, using the pixel's own raw `(x, y)` integer coordinates (not normalized UV — grain's cell size is meant to be a fixed pixel scale).
- **GPU (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`)**: a direct WGSL port (`grainHash`/`grainValueNoise`/`grainDelta`) reading a new dedicated `Grain` uniform buffer at binding 16 (same reasoning Vignette's own binding 15 needed one — `Adjustments` has zero spare padding left). Computed inline in `fs_final` using the same integer `coord` already computed at the top of the pass (not `in.position.xy` directly, which carries WebGPU's own half-pixel fragment-center offset and would have desynced the CPU/GPU noise phase by half a pixel — caught before it ever became a real CPU/GPU mismatch, not after). **A real, if trivial, bug caught by `npm run check` before ever reaching the app**: a doc comment inside the WGSL template-literal string used markdown backticks around the word `sin`, and a literal backtick inside a JS template literal terminates the string early — everything after silently became bare JS being parsed as WGSL-flavored nonsense. Fixed by dropping the markdown formatting; worth remembering for any future WGSL doc comment in this file.
- **Bind-group entry list** (`fs_final`'s own, at binding 16) — the same bind group named in its own comment as having missed a real binding twice already this session — double-checked against the WGSL body's actual `grain.amount`/`.size`/`.roughness` reads before adding, and the comment updated to note this would have been a third near-miss.
- **6 new hand-derived Rust tests**: `grain_hash(0.0, 0.0)` collapses to exactly 0.0 (a real closed-form point on the hash, `sin(0)=0` exactly); amount=0 exact passthrough; determinism (same coordinate, same delta, across repeated calls — pins down the "static, not re-randomized" contract explicitly); at roughness=100 and size=100, two different pixel coordinates in the SAME 6px lattice cell produce the EXACT SAME delta (size's whole visible effect, hand-derivable: `floor(0/6) == floor(3/6)`, `floor(7/6) != floor(0/6)`); roughness=0 vs 100 generally disagree at a real point; an end-to-end `apply_edit_stack` passthrough check. `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 127/127 passing.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit), **deliberately scoped to the error signal only** — the Vignette and Clarity/Texture slices both already root-caused the crop-and-measure visual check's own unreliability to a window-occlusion/backgrounded-canvas-readback artifact unrelated to product correctness (confirmed twice, via `document.visibilityState` flipping to `"hidden"` mid-run and via internally-inconsistent repeated-sample results on a genuinely unchanged state), so repeating that specific technique a third time wasn't pursued. Zero WGSL compile errors and zero uncaptured GPU validation errors across baseline plus four parameter combinations including every extreme (amount/size/roughness all at 100, amount=100 with size/roughness both at 0, and back to amount=0) — directly exercises the exact risk this op's own binding-16 addition carries, and it never fired.

## M3 — Vignette: post-crop-style radial falloff (2026-08-08)

Next unbuilt M3 item after Clarity & Texture. Unlike every spatial op shipped so far this session (Dehaze, Texture, Clarity), Vignette needs NO neighboring-pixel data — it's a pure per-pixel function of a pixel's own UV coordinate, so it folds directly into the existing final pass/loop (both `fs_final` in `DevelopCanvas.svelte` and the final per-pixel loop in `apply_edit_stack`) rather than adding any new GPU pass, texture, or restructured CPU buffer.

- **Three controls** (Amount -100..100, Midpoint 0..100 default 50, Feather 0..100 default 50), matching real Lightroom's own sign convention (negative darkens, positive lightens) and general shape. **Named, deferred simplification**: every vignette here is an aspect-corrected ELLIPSE matching the image's own aspect ratio — real Lightroom's Roundness slider (blending toward a more rectangular shape) isn't implemented, and Style variants (Highlight Priority/Color Priority/Paint Overlay) aren't either; this is the single natural-falloff style only.
- **Formula**: normalize each pixel's aspect-corrected distance from center by the center-to-corner distance (so `midpoint`/`feather` are always relative to "how far out toward the corner," regardless of aspect ratio), run it through `smoothstep(inner, outer, normDist)` where `inner`/`outer` are derived from midpoint/feather (nudged apart by a small epsilon so `smoothstep` never hits its degenerate equal-edges case at midpoint=100 or feather=0), then scale a multiplicative brightness factor by `amount`. `amount=0` is an exact passthrough by construction (the function returns exactly 1.0 without even touching the geometry) — same discipline every other op's identity value already gets.
- **CPU (`develop_engine.rs`)**: a new `Vignette` struct + flat-field parser (mirrors `split_toning`'s "fall back to identity on partial/corrupt payload" contract, but non-nested since there's no natural per-element repetition in three named fields) and a `vignette_factor(uv, aspect, &Vignette) -> f32` helper, reusing the already-in-scope `aspect` variable (`height/width`) masks already compute. A new `smoothstep` helper was added (Rust's std has no builtin) — deliberately the exact same `t*t*(3-2t)` formula WGSL's own built-in computes, so the CPU and GPU falloff curves match rather than merely approximate each other. `uv` is now computed unconditionally in the final loop (previously only inside the `!masks.is_empty()` branch) since Vignette needs it regardless of whether any mask exists.
- **GPU (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`)**: a new small dedicated `Vignette` uniform struct/buffer at binding 15 (Adjustments has zero spare padding floats left after Dehaze/Texture/Clarity already claimed them — same reasoning that gave Split Toning its own buffer). Computed inline inside `fs_final` using `in.uv` (already available, no new vertex-stage plumbing) and `textureDimensions(gradedTex)` for aspect — using WGSL's own built-in `smoothstep` directly rather than porting the CPU's hand-rolled helper. **Extra care taken on the bind-group entry list specifically**: this exact bind group (`fs_final`'s own) has already missed a real binding twice before this session (`srcSampler` during Dehaze's own debugging, then the `Adjustments` uniform in Texture/Clarity's V-passes, caught by that slice's design review) — binding 15 was double-checked against the WGSL body's actual `vignette.amount`/`.midpoint`/`.feather` reads before being added, and the surrounding comment now explicitly names this bind group's history so a future addition doesn't repeat it.
- **Not gated by the shared `spatialOpsInputsKey` dirty-key cache** — same treatment `dehaze_amount` already gets and for the same reason: Vignette only affects `fs_final`'s own cheap output, not any of the expensive maps computed inside that gated block, and `fs_final` already runs unconditionally every render regardless of the dirty key's state.
- **5 new hand-derived Rust tests** on `vignette_factor` directly: amount=0 exact passthrough regardless of geometry; the dead-center pixel is exactly unaffected whenever midpoint is positive (smoothstep's own zero-at-edge0 property); a hand-derived corner case at midpoint=0/feather=100 where `smoothstep(0,1,1)=1.0` exactly, reducing the factor to a clean `1 + amount/100`; a sign-of-amount monotonicity check at a real intermediate point; and one end-to-end `apply_edit_stack` test confirming a real image's corner darkens while its exact center stays untouched. `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 121/121 passing.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit): zero WGSL/GPU validation errors across every amount/midpoint/feather combination tested — directly exercises the exact bind-group-completeness risk named above, and it never fired. **The visual/numeric crop-and-measure confirmation itself was unreliable and abandoned**, not silently accepted: sampled region means were internally inconsistent in a way that ruled out real image content as the explanation (e.g. `backToBaseline`, sampled after resetting Amount to 0, came back byte-identical across all six sampled regions to the immediately-preceding `lightened` sample at Amount=100 — impossible if the frame had actually re-rendered) — the same window-occlusion/backgrounded-canvas-readback artifact already root-caused once this session during Clarity/Texture's own empirical pass (confirmed there via `document.visibilityState` flipping to `"hidden"` mid-run). Given that artifact is now understood and reproduced identically on a second, unrelated formula, further harness iterations on this specific technique were not pursued — the WGSL is a deliberate line-by-line port of the independently-tested CPU formula (down to reusing WGSL's own built-in `smoothstep`), and the zero-errors-across-every-parameter-combination result already directly covers this op's actual historical risk (bind-group completeness), which the visual check would not have added confidence to beyond what the Rust tests already established for the math itself.

## M3 — Clarity & Texture: local-contrast sliders (2026-08-08)

Next unbuilt M3 item after Dehaze: "...clarity/texture, vignette, grain." Asked the user how deep to scope this given Dehaze's own precedent as "likely its own multi-slice arc" — **user chose to build it the same way as real Lightroom**: two genuine local-contrast (unsharp-mask-on-luminance) sliders, -100..100, not a cheap per-pixel approximation. Same op family as Dehaze (needs neighboring-pixel data, not a pure per-pixel remap), so this slice reuses Dehaze's exact architecture rather than inventing a new one: **Texture -> Clarity -> Dehaze**, inserted right before Dehaze in both the CPU (`develop_engine.rs`) and GPU (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`) pipelines, matching Lightroom's own slider order and grouping all three spatial ops together.

- **Design reviewed before implementation, and it caught two real bugs.** (1) The first-draft formula reconstructed each pixel via a luma-ratio rescale (`scale = newLuma / max(luma, epsilon)`, `rgb *= scale`) — algebraically clean but numerically unsound: `graded` is never clamped between ops (Contrast alone already pushes near-black channels negative), so at `luma <= 0` the epsilon floor on the denominator combined with a still-very-negative numerator produces a huge, hue-scrambling `scale` the final clamp-to-[0,1] can't undo (chroma is already corrupted before clamping ever runs). Fixed by switching to an **additive** delta instead: push luma by `(luma - blurred) * (amount/100)` and add that SAME delta to all three channels, no division at all — exact, and `newC - newLuma == C - luma` for every channel always, so chroma is preserved exactly regardless of how extreme `graded`'s values get. This also fixed a second, subtler issue the review traced from the first: the ratio version wasn't exactly identity at `amount=0` for a near-zero-luma pixel (`scale = luma/epsilon != 1`), which would have made the CPU path (which skips the op entirely at `amount=0`) and the GPU path (which the design always runs unconditionally once the shared block is dirty) genuinely disagree at the one point they're supposed to always match. (2) The first-draft bind-group entry lists for the two new "V" passes (`fs_texture_v`/`fs_clarity_v`) omitted binding 2 (the `Adjustments` uniform) even though both WGSL entry points read `adj.texture_amount`/`adj.clarity_amount` — the exact same class of bug (a real dependency missed on the bind group's entry list) already caught once this session for Dehaze's own final pass (`srcSampler`).
- **CPU (`develop_engine.rs`)**: one new function, `apply_local_contrast(graded, width, height, radius, amount)`, reusing `separable_mean_filter` completely unchanged (already generic on radius, no new filter primitive needed). Called twice in `apply_edit_stack` right after Pass 1 builds `graded` — Texture first, then Clarity (each independently skipped at `amount=0.0`, same exact-passthrough discipline as Dehaze) — before Dehaze's own maps are computed from the (now texture/clarity-adjusted) `graded` buffer. Fixed radii, same "fix the knob" choice as Dehaze's own constants, same named/deferred "not resolution-scaled" limitation: `TEXTURE_RADIUS=6`, `CLARITY_RADIUS=24`.
- **GPU (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`)**: 4 new WGSL entry points (`fs_texture_h`/`fs_texture_v`/`fs_clarity_h`/`fs_clarity_v`, a direct port of the CPU formula), 2 new rebindable bindings (`lcRgbInput` binding 13, `lcBlurInput` binding 14), 3 new textures (`textureBlurScratchTex`, `textureAdjustedTex`, `clarityBlurScratchTex` — kept as separate dedicated textures per op, not one shared scratch texture reused across both, even though pass ordering alone would make that safe: matches every other Dehaze filter stage's own one-texture-per-stage convention, so a future pass reordering can't silently corrupt output with no validation error to catch it). **Clarity's own V pass writes its output directly back into the existing `gradedTex`, overwriting it in place**, rather than adding a third "final graded" texture and repointing Dehaze's three existing `gradedTex` consumers (`fs_atm_reduce`'s first pass, `fs_min_channel`, `fs_final`) — sound because WebGPU render passes within one command encoder execute strictly in recorded order, the exact same guarantee `gradedTex` already relied on before this slice (written once by `fs_grade`, read three times later in the same frame). The shared dirty-key cache (renamed `dehazeInputsKey`/`dehazeKey` -> `spatialOpsInputsKey`/`spatialOpsKey`, now gating three ops' shared ~13-pass block instead of just Dehaze's) gained `texture`/`clarity` to its JSON key — asymmetric with `dehaze_amount`, which deliberately stays OUT of the key (only the cheap final blend reads it; texture/clarity write INTO `gradedTex` itself, inside the expensive block, so they must invalidate the cache).
- **6 new hand-derived Rust tests**: amount=0 exact passthrough; a uniform field is unaffected by ANY amount (box-mean of a constant is that same constant, so `luma-blurred==0` everywhere); a hand-derived 3-column delta at radius=1 accounting for edge-clamping's asymmetric per-column weighting (not just a naive 3-way split); the same case confirming amount scales the delta linearly; an end-to-end `apply_edit_stack` passthrough check. `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 116/116 passing.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (`report_spike_result`-bridged harness, removed before commit): zero WGSL compile errors and zero uncaptured GPU validation errors across ~20 renders spanning every amount combination tested (100, 50, -100, 0, and Clarity/Texture together) — directly exercises the exact bind-group-completeness question the design review raised, and never triggered the error overlay. Clarity's numeric behavior was confirmed correct via an in-page crop-and-measure check (200x200 canvas crop, luminance mean/stddev): positive Clarity raised local-contrast stddev, negative Clarity lowered it (smoothing), and returning to amount=0 reproduced the prior baseline's stddev bit-for-bit. **One harness limitation surfaced and was root-caused, not silently worked around**: canvas-crop samples intermittently read back as fully black (mean=0, stddev=0) — investigated by re-sampling the SAME steady state (zero slider changes at all) repeatedly, which reproduced the identical black/non-black alternation and showed `document.visibilityState` flipping to `"hidden"` mid-run, confirming this is a canvas-capture race tied to the Tauri window running unattended/intermittently-occluded in the background, not a rendering defect (every sample, black or not, had `error: null`, and the WGSL side is an exact, reviewed line-by-line port of the already-tested CPU formula).

## Develop info bar (filename) + friendly missing-source-file error (2026-08-07)

Two small, related user requests. (1) Show the current image's filename somewhere near the bottom of Develop — clarified via `AskUserQuestion`: a thin bar directly under the Develop canvas, above the Filmstrip, Develop-only (Library already has this via `MetadataPanel`). (2) Fix `"could not read source file from disk: No such file or directory (os error 2)"` surfacing as a raw, cryptic OS error string.

- **Root-caused the second report to a real, concrete trigger**, not a hypothetical: `PreviewCacheError::Io` (`preview_cache.rs`) wraps `std::io::Error` via `#[from]`, and its derived `Display` interpolates the raw OS error string directly. Confirmed three cataloged images (ids 2, 3, 5) in the real catalog point at files under an old session's now-cleaned-up scratchpad directory and no longer exist on disk — genuine dangling references (test debris from earlier empirical-verification sessions that imported from session-scoped scratchpad paths), not a code bug in the reference itself.
