Missing/moved/renamed/offline-drive source files are a normal, expected-to-happen-eventually reality for any photo catalog — the error *message* shown for it just needed to be actionable, not the raw errno.
- **Fixed at the root, not per-surface**: `PreviewCacheError::user_message()` inspects the wrapped `io::Error`'s `.kind()` — `ErrorKind::NotFound` gets a clean, actionable message ("Source photo not found -- it may have been moved, renamed, or deleted outside the app, or is on a disconnected drive."), every other kind falls through unchanged to the existing technical `Display` message (left untouched — still useful internally/in tests, where the precise error is more useful than a friendly one). `get_develop_preview`/`get_develop_full_preview` (the two Tauri commands that can hit this) switched from `.map_err(|e| e.to_string())` to `.map_err(|e| e.user_message())` — no frontend change needed, `DevelopCanvas.svelte`'s existing `.overlay.error` display already shows whatever string the command rejects with.
- **Explicitly scoped to the Develop-open path** (named, not silently narrowed): Library grid thumbnails already degrade gracefully for a missing thumbnail file (a plain broken-image state, not a crash) and `export.rs`'s own per-item `ExportResult.error` already prevents one missing file from aborting a whole batch export — neither currently shows this same raw string, so this fix covers the actual reported symptom without needing to touch either already-non-crashing path.
- **Info bar**: a new small `DevelopInfoBar.svelte` — just the filename (derived from `developImagePath`'s own basename, no new state) for this slice, matching "smallest real instance first" (no dimensions/file size/EXIF — those already live in `MetadataPanel` for when the user wants them). Styled to match the titlebar's own thin-bar conventions rather than inventing a new visual language.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit): opened Develop for the real test image, confirmed the info bar showed its correct filename (`IMG_9755.jpg`); opened Develop for one of the catalog's own real dangling entries (image id 2, whose file no longer exists), confirmed the friendly message appeared verbatim in the existing error overlay, not the raw OS string.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 111/111 passing (2 new tests: a genuinely-missing source path produces the friendly message while its own `Display`/technical message stays unchanged; a different `io::ErrorKind` still falls through to the existing technical message, confirming `user_message()` only special-cases the one kind with an obvious explanation).

## Fix: thumbnail-regen/flush-save race hazard (2026-08-07)

User reported a photo's thumbnail seeming to go missing/not reflect an edit, while a separate Dehaze WGSL bug (fixed in the same session, see below) was independently confirmed as the actual cause of what they were seeing at the time (a broken Develop-canvas render, not a Library/Filmstrip thumbnail issue). While investigating, found a real, code-verified hazard worth fixing regardless: `openDevelop`, `switchModule`, and `handleExportClick` each fire `flushEditStack()` (writes the current edit stack to the catalog) immediately followed by `regenerateThumbnailFor()` (a separate IPC call whose own Rust command re-reads the edit stack fresh from the catalog, per its own doc comment) — WITHOUT awaiting the first call. Neither Tauri's own command dispatch nor the underlying SQLite write is guaranteed to land before the very next command's own read starts, so the regen could in principle read a stale, pre-edit stack.

- **Not independently confirmed as a reproduced user-visible symptom** — an attempt to reproduce one via the real running app was confounded by reusing an identical edit-stack value (the same Dehaze amount) across consecutive test runs, which correctly produces an identical, unchanged content-addressed thumbnail path regardless of ordering (a false alarm from the test's own methodology, not evidence of the race). Once corrected (a genuinely different value each run), the mechanism worked correctly even before this fix.
- **Fixed anyway, on precedent**: this is the same class of bug ("a write and a dependent read fired without awaiting the write") already found and fixed once this session for `flushEditStack()` itself (its own `persistTimer`-gated write, fixed as part of the Reset-button slice) — worth closing here too rather than leaving a real, if unconfirmed, hazard in place. All three call sites now `await flushEditStack()` before `regenerateThumbnailFor()`.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 103/103 passing (frontend-only change).

## M3 — Dehaze: dark-channel-prior haze removal (2026-08-07)

Next unbuilt M3 item after Split Toning: "Tone curve, HSL/color mixer, split toning, **dehaze**, clarity/texture, vignette, grain." Every op shipped before this was strictly per-pixel on both the GPU (one WGSL fragment shader, one render pass) and CPU (one `enumerate_pixels_mut` loop) sides. A textbook Dehaze (He et al. 2009's dark-channel-prior haze removal) genuinely needs spatial/whole-image awareness — a local minimum filter for the dark channel, a whole-image reduction for atmospheric light, a transmission map — which no prior op needed. Asked the user directly whether to scope this as (a) a per-pixel approximation matching every other op's architecture, (b) true dark-channel-prior requiring genuinely new multi-pass architecture, or (c) skip for now — **user chose (b)**, explicitly informed this was a materially bigger, riskier addition than any M3 op shipped so far.

- **A Plan-subagent design review caught real, concrete algorithm bugs in the first draft before any code was written** — not stylistic nitpicks: (1) estimating atmospheric light via independent per-channel maxima can synthesize a color no pixel in the image actually has and is unboundedly sensitive to a single outlier channel — fixed with an argmax-by-luminance reduction that always propagates a real pixel's whole RGB triple; (2) the draft's "collapse the cross-channel min first, then divide by a scalar representative of A" ordering is structurally wrong, not just imprecise — He et al.'s own formula divides `I^c/A^c` per channel *before* the windowed min, and doing it after has already discarded the information needed (a concrete counterexample with a non-neutral `A=(0.1,0.9,0.3)` produces both a different value AND a different winning channel) — fixed by reordering: compute atmospheric light first, normalize per-channel, then take the windowed min; (3) a naive reference-equality dirty-key cache design would have silently never fired, since `masks`/`toneCurvePoints`/`hslBands`/`splitToning` are all rebuilt via `$derived` on every `editStack` change regardless of which op changed — fixed with a value-based (`JSON.stringify`) key, correctly scoped to exclude masks/selectedMaskId/showMaskOverlay (which don't affect the expensive passes) so an unthrottled mask-handle drag doesn't retrigger the whole chain every frame.
- **Algorithm** (exact same formula on both WGSL and Rust CPU, no LUT — same "closed-form both sides" precedent HSL/Split Toning already established): atmospheric light `A` via an 8x8-block argmax-by-luminance reduction (GPU) / a single full-image scan (CPU, no bounded-pass constraint there); normalized min-channel `min_c(I^c/A^c)`; a separable 15x15 box-min for the dark channel; raw transmission `1 - 0.95*darkChannel`; a separable 9x9 box-mean refinement (standing in for He et al.'s edge-preserving guided filter — named limitation: mild haloing near strong contrast edges, explicitly deferred); recovery `(I-A)/max(t,0.1) + A`, blended by a single 0-100 Amount slider (0 = exact passthrough, both sides skip the entire expensive computation at amount=0, not just discard the result).
- **The GPU side needed a genuinely new multi-pipeline render graph** — this codebase's first (previously: exactly one `GPURenderPipeline`, one `beginRenderPass`, in the whole file). 8 distinct pipelines now: `fs_grade` (the pre-existing global chain, redirected from the swapchain into a new `gradedTex` intermediate), a generic argmax-by-luminance reduction (`fs_atm_reduce`, reused across ~4 passes at 8x8-block reduction each, rebinding to progressively smaller textures), `fs_min_channel`/`fs_min_h`/`fs_min_v` (the dark-channel separable min-filter, folding in the raw-transmission step), `fs_mean_h`/`fs_mean_v` (the transmission-refinement separable mean-filter), and `fs_final` (recovery + amount blend, then the existing mask loop, unchanged). All new intermediates read via `textureLoad` (not `textureSample`) with manual edge clamping — `r32float` isn't filterable by default in WebGPU, and `textureLoad` sidesteps the sampler-format question entirely; `gradedTex` is `rgba16float`, not `rgba8unorm`, avoiding a new 8-bit quantization step that didn't exist in the old single-pass shader.
- **A real, hard-to-localize bug found and fixed during empirical verification, not caught by any static check**: the WGSL compiled clean and every bind group created without error, but the very first render still failed with a generic `setBindGroup: invalid bind group` — no indication of which of the 8 passes. Standard WebGPU error scopes (`pushErrorScope`/`popErrorScope`) around every bind-group creation call found nothing (all valid), which turned out to be the real clue: the failure was in *using* a bind group, not creating one. Bisection (temporarily skipping the 7 new passes, testing the final pass alone) isolated it to `fs_final`'s own bind group. Root cause: `fs_final`'s mask loop — copied unchanged from the old `fs_main` — samples `brushMasks` via `textureSampleLevel(brushMasks, srcSampler, ...)`, which references `srcSampler` (binding 0). The new bind group's entry list assumed `fs_final` needed none of the original bindings 0/1/5/6/7 (since `srcTexture`/`curveLut`/`hslBands`/`splitToning` genuinely moved into `fs_grade`), but the mask loop's own pre-existing `srcSampler` dependency doesn't go away just because Dehaze was inserted above it. Fixed by adding binding 0 back to the final pass's entries. Along the way, added two permanent, non-debugging improvements this component never had: a `device.addEventListener("uncapturederror", ...)` listener (most WebGPU errors are reported asynchronously, not as a catchable JS exception, so this component previously had zero way to surface one outside the webview's own inaccessible devtools console) and a `shaderModule.getCompilationInfo()` check (a *separate* WebGPU error category from validation errors, surfaced only via this async call) — together they close the exact "nothing signals anything is wrong" gap that made this specific bug so slow to localize.
- **The Rust CPU side needed a real restructuring of `apply_edit_stack`, not an inserted call the way Split Toning's own single-scalar op was** — a pixel's dark-channel/transmission value needs its *neighbors'* already-graded value, which the existing single in-place `enumerate_pixels_mut` loop can't provide without a race. Restructured into explicit passes over intermediate buffers (a `graded: Vec<[f32;3]>` buffer, then atmospheric light, then the min/mean filter passes, then a final recovery+mask loop) — skipped entirely at `amount=0` for both correctness (an exact passthrough, not a numerically-near-zero one) and performance (most exports won't use Dehaze). Two new filter primitives, both export-time-acceptable at this app's real 4032×3024 test image: a naive per-pixel 15-tap min filter for the dark channel (~365M comparisons total, well under a second in release mode — Van Herk/Gil-Werman's O(1)-amortized monotonic-deque technique would be the textbook-correct answer at a much larger radius, not needed here) and an exact O(1)-per-pixel sliding-window sum accumulator for the 9-tap mean filter (correct because sums, unlike min/max, can be "un-added").
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit): confirmed the full 8-pipeline render graph renders without error across repeated Dehaze-amount changes (not just the first frame); confirmed the persisted `dehaze` op via `getEditStack`; real export at `amount=40` vs `amount=0`, pixel-sampled against each other at four points on the real source photo — three showed real, physically sensible shifts (e.g. one region moved `srgb(178,167,113)→srgb(157,142,75)`, both darkening AND increasing inter-channel spread, consistent with haze removal's own contrast-increasing effect) and one showed no change (a region apparently already close to the atmospheric-light estimate itself, where the recovery formula is near-identity — not a bug, a real property of the algorithm).
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 109/109 passing (6 new hand-derived tests: `amount=0` exact passthrough on a real multi-pixel image; a hand-derived recovery value at `amount=100` using a crafted sky-patch-plus-uniform-scene test image chosen so `A=(1,1,1)` exactly, eliminating division complexity from the hand computation; a pixel already at atmospheric light recovers unchanged; the atmospheric-light reduction picks a real pixel's triple, not a synthesized per-channel-max color; unit tests for both new separable filter primitives against small hand-computed arrays).

## Develop panel: "Reset" button (2026-08-05)

User asked to "add reset button to edit zone" — clarified via `AskUserQuestion` (whole-panel reset vs. per-Split-Toning-zone reset vs. per-section reset) that this means one button reverting **every** adjustment and mask on the current photo back to default in one shot, matching real Lightroom's own Develop-module Reset, not a scoped per-section reset.

- **Trivial by construction, once traced through the existing code**: `editStack` is `{schema_version, ops: []}` and every adjustment/tool/mask this app has is one entry in that same flat `ops` array. A full reset is just "clear `ops`, keep `schema_version`" (`resetEditStack`, new in `develop.js`) — every section's own getter (`getToneCurvePoints`, `getHslBands`, `opValue`, `listMasks`, …) already falls back to its own identity default when its op is absent, so nothing downstream needed new wiring; the reactive plumbing built for every prior slice handles it for free.
- **A real, pre-existing persistence bug found and fixed along the way, not just worked around.** The plan modeled the new handler on `handleMaskDeleted`'s existing shape (mutate `editStack`, then call `flushEditStack()` immediately, bypassing the usual 250ms slider debounce). Empirical verification against the real running app caught that this doesn't actually persist: `flushEditStack()`'s write was gated behind `if (persistTimer !== null)` — correct for every slider-driven caller (which always sets `persistTimer` via `setTimeout` right before a later flush), but silently wrong for any handler that mutates `editStack` directly and expects an immediate write, since neither `handleMaskDeleted` nor the new Reset handler ever sets that timer first. The first empirical pass showed the in-memory state correctly reset (`editStackAfterOps: 0`) while the catalog's real persisted row still had all 5 original ops (`persistedOps: 5`) — a genuine, verified defect, not a suspected one. **This means mask deletions had the same latent bug all along**: a deleted mask's removal was never actually written to the catalog unless some unrelated later slider edit happened to also flush before quitting, so a deleted mask could silently reappear after a full app quit and reopen. Fixed at the root: `flushEditStack()` now always persists the current stack whenever a Develop image is open, regardless of whether a timer happened to be pending — harmless (an idempotent re-write of unchanged data) for its other callers (`openDevelop`, `switchModule`, `handleExportClick`, all of which only ever call it when a timer legitimately might be pending), and correct for the two immediate-mutation callers that don't set one. Re-verified empirically after the fix: a second pass (triggered by Vite HMR reloading after the source edit) confirmed `persistedOps: 0` against the real catalog.
- **UI**: a small `.panel-header` row at the top of `DevelopPanel.svelte`, above "Basic" — "Edit" label + a "Reset" button, disabled when there's nothing to reset (`editStack.ops.length === 0`, avoiding a no-op confirmation dialog, the same guard shape the titlebar's own "Remove" button already uses). Gated behind the existing `ConfirmDialog.svelte` component (already used for "Remove from catalog," no changes needed to it) since this is destructive and can't be undone. Styled with the same destructive-action color this codebase already uses elsewhere (`MaskEditorPanel.svelte`'s `.delete` button, `ConfirmDialog.svelte`'s own `.danger` confirm button) rather than inventing a new visual language for "this is destructive."
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit), reusing the real catalog's own leftover multi-op test state (a luminance-range mask, a linear-gradient mask, exposure, HSL bands, and a tone-curve op — 5 ops total, genuine debris from earlier unrelated testing sessions, itself a real stand-in for "a photo with real edits"): confirmed the button starts disabled on first load's edit count check, becomes enabled once edits exist, clicking it opens the confirm dialog, confirming clears the in-memory edit stack to empty AND (after the fix above) the real persisted catalog row, resets `selectedMaskId`/`activeTool` to `null`, and every section's UI correctly falls back to its own identity default (tone curve back to the straight `[{0,0},{1,1}]` line, all 8 HSL bands back to zero). Catalog restored to its original pre-test state afterward.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 99/99 passing (no Rust changes; the fix was frontend-only, and every op this reset touches was already correctly handled by the Rust side for the "absent op" case, since that's the exact state a never-edited photo already produces).

## M3 — Eyedropper pickers for Tone Curve, HSL/Color, Split Toning (2026-08-05)

User asked for a "color picker" on the three just-shipped M3 color tools. Genuinely ambiguous across all three (Split Toning has a real absolute-color concept; HSL's sliders are relative shifts with no single absolute color; Tone Curve has no color concept at all), so scope was clarified with two direct `AskUserQuestion`s rather than assumed: **eyedropper (sample from the photo)**, not a swatch/wheel picker, for Split Toning and HSL; and for Tone Curve, a targeted-adjustment-style **click-to-insert-a-curve-point** at the sampled pixel's own tonal value (the closest real equivalent this panel's scope has to a "color picker"). Final shape: four new click-driven interactions — Split Toning Shadows eyedropper, Split Toning Highlights eyedropper, HSL band-identify eyedropper (navigation only, no edit-stack write — HSL's own sliders are relative shifts, not an absolute color a sample could set), Tone Curve point-insert.

- **Generalizes the color-range mask's existing "re-sample" click-to-sample gesture and state machine, rather than reinventing it** — reviewed against the real code by a Plan subagent before implementation. One new `activeTool === "eyedropper"` value with a companion `eyedropperTarget` (`+page.svelte`), self-cleaning via the same `$effect` pattern `colorRangeResampleTarget` already uses. Deliberately deviates from that precedent in one place: `eyedropperTarget` is never threaded into `DevelopCanvas.svelte` as a prop, because none of the four destinations need the canvas component to know *which* one is waiting — unlike color-range resampling, where `DevelopCanvas` itself branches between two callback props. All four share one new `onEyedropperSampled` callback instead.
- **A real, previously-unnoticed gap the review caught**: no RGB→HSL conversion existed anywhere in JavaScript — every prior use in `develop.js` only went the other direction (slider values → GPU uniform data). Added one shared `rgbToHsl(r,g,b)`, mirroring `rgb_to_hsl` in `develop_engine.rs` exactly (same `(max+min)/2` lightness, same `rem_euclid` hue wraparound) — all three sampling-based eyedroppers (Split Toning hue+saturation, HSL band-identify hue, Tone Curve lightness) are trivial projections of this one function, not three ad hoc conversions.
- **HSL band-identify deliberately does NOT reuse the render path's `hsl_band_weight` raised-cosine blend formula** — that answers "how much should up to 2 adjacent bands blend," a different question from this feature's "which single band wins." Added a separate `nearestHslBand(hueDeg)` (nearest-by-circular-distance). Since `HSL_BAND_CENTERS_DEG` are evenly spaced 45° apart, nearest-by-distance and highest-blend-weight provably always agree — this is exact for a single-winner answer, not an approximation of the render-path formula.
- **Tone Curve's point-insertion logic was lifted out of `ToneCurveEditor.svelte`, not duplicated.** Its private `addPointAt` (the `MAX_CURVE_POINTS` cap + `MIN_X_GAP` divide-by-zero guard) was the only place this logic lived; the eyedropper needed the identical rule reachable from `+page.svelte`. Exported as `insertToneCurvePoint(points, x, y)` + `MIN_TONE_CURVE_X_GAP` from `develop.js`, and `ToneCurveEditor.svelte`'s own click-on-graph gesture now calls the same exported function — removes the only other place `0.001` was hardcoded, eliminating a two-copies-must-stay-in-sync risk rather than accepting it. The inserted point's y is seeded at the curve's own current value at that x (`sampleCurveLut(buildToneCurveLut(...), x)`), so insertion alone never changes the curve's visible shape until the new point is dragged.
- **One real, named limitation, not silently shipped**: every eyedropper here samples the *original decoded source* pixel, not the live-graded preview (the same accepted limitation `sampleSourcePixel`'s own doc comment already states for color-range masking). For Split Toning/HSL, the consequence is bounded (mild hue drift on a heavily-graded image). For Tone Curve it's sharper: the new point's **x** — the entire semantic payload of the feature — comes from the ungraded pixel, while its y is seeded from the already-graded curve, so a heavily exposure/contrast-graded image could insert a point at a noticeably different x than where that tone visually sits post-grade. Named in a code comment at the call site, not treated as a bug to silently work around.
- **UI**: one eyedropper button per Split Toning zone (next to that zone's existing live swatch); one eyedropper button for the whole HSL section (a new row above the 8 band groups, not inside the section's `<summary>` — nesting an interactive button in an already-clickable disclosure toggle risks the click bubbling into it); one eyedropper button for the whole Tone Curve section (a new row above the curve graph, not inside `ToneCurveEditor.svelte` itself, which has no knowledge of `+page.svelte`-level tool-activation state and stays a purely controlled `{points, onChange}` component). All three reuse `MaskEditorPanel.svelte`'s exact `.resample` button markup/CSS and eyedropper SVG icon verbatim — this codebase's established "same icon vocabulary across the app" practice. HSL's band-identify result drives a transient `highlightedHslBand` (`+page.svelte`, a fixed 1500ms auto-clear timeout, same debounce idiom as the existing `persistTimer`) that scrolls the matching band into view (`.panel` is genuinely `overflow-y: auto`, and a band near the bottom — magenta — can sit outside the visible scroll area when the section first opens) and applies a brief outline highlight.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit): toggled each of the four eyedropper buttons via `handleEyedropperToggle`, dispatched real `PointerEvent`s against the real canvas element at computed screen coordinates, and read back real component state. Split Toning Shadows/Highlights independently set that zone's hue/saturation to the sampled pixel's own HSL values (e.g. a brownish-orange source pixel produced `hue≈25.5°, saturation≈32%` for Shadows while Highlights, sampled separately, landed at `hue≈49.8°, saturation≈30.2%` — computed by hand from the same pixel's real RGB and matched to within normal decoder-rounding, confirming `rgbToHsl`'s formula is correct against real sampled data, not just unit-test inputs) with the other zone and Balance left untouched, and `activeTool` correctly reset to `null` after the one-shot commit. HSL band-identify correctly picked `"orange"` as nearest band for that same pixel's hue, left `hslBands` byte-for-byte unchanged (confirming the navigation-only claim empirically, not just by inspection), and the highlight cleared itself after the 1500ms timeout. Tone Curve inserted a new point at the sampled pixel's lightness with y equal to the identity curve's own value there to within LUT-quantization precision (`x≈0.5157, y≈0.5157`) — confirming the "no visible shape change on insert" claim empirically. As in prior slices, the real catalog's leftover ops from earlier unrelated testing (on `image_versions.id=4`) were temporarily cleared for a clean, unconfounded pass and restored to their exact original state afterward.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 103/103 passing (full suite re-run for regression; no Rust changes in this slice — pure JS/UI feature, no new GPU buffer).

## M3 — Split Toning (2026-08-05)

Next unbuilt M3 item after HSL / Color Mixer: classic split toning — a Shadows zone and a Highlights zone, each with an absolute tint Hue (0-360) and Saturation (0-100, tint strength), plus a shared Balance (-100..100) shifting where the shadow/highlight crossover sits in the tonal range (5 parameters total). Modern 3-zone Color Grading (adds Midtones + per-zone Luminance) is an explicit, named follow-up, not this slice. Slots in as the new final global-pass step: exposure → contrast → saturation → tone curve → HSL → **split toning**, still before masks (confirmed unchanged, zero mask-formula edits needed). Unlike Tone Curve/HSL, `DevelopPanel.svelte` had no placeholder for this — a genuinely new section, inserted right after "HSL / Color."

- **Design reviewed before implementation, and it caught a real bug before it was ever written.** My own draft approach applied the Shadow and Highlight tints as two *sequential* blends (shadow-then-highlight, or the reverse) — the review proved this is order-dependent (the result differs by a real cross term) whenever both zone weights are nonzero, which given this design's full-range smoothstep transition is true for nearly every pixel, not a rare edge case. Fixed with a single simultaneous 3-way convex combination instead (`rgb*(1-a_sh-a_hi) + tint_sh*a_sh + tint_hi*a_hi`), proven algebraically order-independent and overshoot-safe: since the two zone weights always sum to exactly 1 and both saturation terms are capped at 1, the three blend weights are provably non-negative and sum to exactly 1 — a genuine convex combination, no clamp needed. It also corrected my own framing on a real, easy-to-miss detail: **three** null-guard lists needed the new GPU buffer added, not two (`applyBitmapToGpu`, `loadImage`, and `writeAdjustmentsAndRender`, confirmed by direct grep) — the same class of miss the HSL slice's own review had already flagged once.
- **Reuses HSL's already-shipped `rgb_to_hsl`/`hsl_to_rgb` (Rust) and `rgbToHsl`/`hslToRgb` (WGSL) as-is** — no new color-space math needed. Zone weight is a smoothstep of the pixel's own lightness (`(max+min)/2`, the SAME lightness definition `hsl_to_rgb` requires as input to reconstruct color correctly — using perceptual luma here instead would be colorimetrically wrong, not just a stylistic difference), shifted by Balance: `t = clamp(l + balance/200, 0, 1); weight_highlights = smoothstep(0,1,t)`. Smoothstep (not a linear ramp) chosen for the same C¹-continuity reason HSL used raised-cosine over a triangular ramp — no slope-kink seam on a smooth luminance gradient.
- **A confirmed non-risk, named explicitly rather than reflexively porting over HSL's own mitigation**: HSL's `chroma_fade` term (suppressing hue-shift near gray, since hue is a ratio of near-equal RGB channels and therefore numerically unstable there) does NOT apply to this formula — split toning never reads a pixel's own hue or saturation, only its lightness, a simple, well-behaved computation with no division-by-small-delta anywhere. Porting `chroma_fade` over anyway would have actively *broken* the feature: tinting near-neutral shadow/highlight tones is split toning's entire purpose (classic teal-orange grading relies on exactly that).
- **A real, deliberate, checked property of the fixed-width transition, named rather than silently discovered later**: at `balance=±100`, the disfavored zone's weight never drops below 0.5 anywhere in `[0,1]` — the crossover midpoint simply sits at one endpoint of the transition. Not a bug; an accepted consequence of choosing a transition that spans the image's full tonal range rather than adding a 6th "width" parameter.
- **New WGSL `@binding(7)` — a dedicated named `struct SplitToning`, not a vec4-array like HSL's `hslBands`/the tone curve's `curveLut`**: 5 named scalars (shadow hue/saturation, highlight hue/saturation, balance) have no natural per-element repetition the way 8 bands or 256 samples do, so this mirrors the existing `Adjustments` struct's own style instead. Confirmed `Adjustments` itself has zero spare capacity for these (its 3 padding floats are alignment padding, not general room) before deciding a new binding was actually needed, not assumed.
- **`develop.js` gets a NESTED (not flat) data model** — `{op: "split_toning", shadows: {hue,saturation}, highlights: {hue,saturation}, balance}` — matching `upsertHslBand`'s own "patch one zone without touching the rest" access pattern, and chosen specifically so a later 3-zone Color Grading follow-up can add a `midtones` key additively rather than needing a flat-naming-scheme restructure.
- **UI**: two zone groups (Shadows, Highlights), each with a live color swatch computed directly from that zone's own current Hue/Saturation slider values (`hsl(hue, saturation%, 50%)`) — deliberately different from HSL's own swatch, which is tied to a *fixed* band-center constant (an identity marker, unrelated to that band's own shift-delta slider values). Split Toning's sliders ARE themselves an absolute color, so the swatch must track live values or it's simply wrong; at saturation=0 it correctly renders mid-gray regardless of hue, a free visual confirmation the zone is currently a no-op. The Hue slider deliberately uses a plain `0..360` unsigned display, NOT HSL's `+`/`-` signed-value idiom — a real, flagged-in-advance copy-paste risk when using the HSL row as a markup template, since Split Toning's Hue is an absolute tint-wheel position, not a relative shift.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit): dragged the real Shadow Hue/Saturation sliders, confirmed the real persisted `split_toning` op via `getEditStack`; confirmed the WGSL pipeline renders without error with non-identity values active; ran a real export and pixel-sampled a genuinely dark region and a genuinely bright region of the real source photo against the true unedited source file — the dark region (lightness ≈0.15, shadow-weight ≈0.94) shifted strongly and warmly toward the orange shadow tint, while the bright region (lightness ≈0.80, shadow-weight ≈0.10) shifted only mildly, in exactly the proportion the formula predicts for each region's own computed weight — not a hard on/off cutoff, matching the design's own full-range smoothstep transition.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 103/103 passing (4 new hand-derived tests: zero-saturation passthrough regardless of hue/balance; a pure-shadow pixel isolating the Shadow zone; a pure-highlight pixel isolating the Highlight zone; and — the direct regression test for the sequential-blending bug the design review caught — a mid-gray pixel with both zones set to deliberately non-complementary hues and unequal saturations, script-verified that the correct simultaneous blend `(172,109,58)` differs by well outside tolerance from what an incorrect sequential blend of the identical inputs would produce `(163,118,67)`).

## M3 — HSL / Color Mixer (2026-08-04)

Next unbuilt M3 item after Tone Curve: 8 named color bands (Red, Orange, Yellow, Green, Aqua, Blue, Purple, Magenta), each with independent Hue/Saturation/Luminance sliders (24 parameters), where a pixel's own hue determines how much each band's adjustment applies — smoothly blended between adjacent bands, not a hard cutoff. The first genuinely new class of computation in this codebase: everything shipped so far (exposure/contrast/saturation, the tone curve's LUT) operates in plain RGB; this is the first real RGB↔HSL/hue-wheel math. Slots in as the new final step of the global pass: exposure → contrast → saturation → tone curve → **HSL**, still before masks (which read the post-global `rgb` as their own accumulator — confirmed unchanged, zero mask-formula edits needed).

- **Design reviewed before implementation**, specifically on the color-science formula (the highest-risk, most novel part). It verified directly against the real code (not assumed) that the existing `Adjustments` uniform struct has zero spare room for 24 new values (confirming a new WGSL binding is required), that no tab-widget precedent exists anywhere in this codebase, and that this project's zero-`svelte-ignore` a11y discipline still holds. It made one deliberate, justified departure from the just-shipped Tone Curve precedent: **no shared LUT this time** — the entire HSL formula is closed-form trig/arithmetic (mod, abs, cos, a few divides, a clamp) with no interpolation-method ambiguity to diverge on between WGSL and Rust, unlike the tone curve's genuinely shape-ambiguous monotonic spline; a LUT here would be unneeded complexity.
- **Standard HSL, not this codebase's own perceptual luma** — the HSL→RGB reconstruction is only mathematically valid for the lightness it was derived from (`(max+min)/2`); substituting the existing `dot(rgb, vec3(0.2126,0.7152,0.0722))` luma (used elsewhere for global Saturation and luminance-range-mask selection, an unrelated concern) would produce colorimetrically wrong output, not just a stylistic difference.
- **Band centers evenly spaced 45° apart starting at Red** (no verifiable source for Adobe's own internal angles, and this is a from-scratch reimplementation, not file-compatible with Lightroom) with a **raised-cosine blend weight** between adjacent bands — chosen over a triangular ramp specifically because it's C¹-continuous (no slope-kink seam at a band center on a continuous hue gradient like sky or skin). Centers being exactly 45° apart means at most 2 bands are ever active at once, and their weights sum to exactly 1 by the cosine identity `cos(x) + cos(π−x) = 0` — no gap, no double-count, no renormalization needed anywhere.
- **All 8 bands combine into one hue/saturation/luminance delta, applied once** (not 8 successive blends) — correct, not just cheaper, since at most 2 weights are ever nonzero. The hue-wraparound pitfall (naively averaging two angles straddling 0°/360°) is avoided entirely by combining **shift deltas**, not resultant angles, and wrapping only once on the summed delta.
- **A real, specific risk identified and given a load-bearing (not defensive) fix**: hue is derived from ratios/differences of near-equal RGB channel values — exactly where 8-bit source-texture quantization noise is largest relative to signal, i.e. in low-saturation regions (skies, skin, walls). Unlike every earlier adjustment (smooth remaps of an already-quantized input), HSL's hue-rotation is a genuinely new mechanism that could amplify that noise into visible mottling. Fixed with a `chroma_fade` term (`clamp(saturation / 0.08, 0, 1)`) that tapers hue-shift and luminance-delta application toward zero exactly where hue becomes numerically unreliable — algorithmic, not a higher-precision-texture architectural change. Also correctly handles achromatic pixels' mathematically-undefined hue (harmless for Saturation, which is naturally self-fading; would be visibly wrong for Luminance without the same fade).
- **New WGSL uniform** `@binding(6) hslBands: array<vec4<f32>, 8>` — one vec4 per band (hue/saturation/luminance/unused padding), device-scoped like `curveLutBuffer` (created once in `initGpu`, rewritten every render, never recreated per image). Both existing null-guard lists (`applyBitmapToGpu`'s and `writeAdjustmentsAndRender`'s) extended — the exact footgun the Tone Curve slice already flagged as easy to miss one of.
- **A WGSL-specific correctness fix, caught before it could ship silently wrong**: WGSL's `%` operator is truncated remainder (sign follows the dividend), not the always-non-negative floor-mod this hue-wraparound math needs — used a small `rem_euclid(a, b) = a - b*floor(a/b)` helper throughout the WGSL port to exactly match Rust's `f32::rem_euclid`, rather than assuming `%` behaves the same in both languages.
- **`develop.js` gets a band-KEYED (not flat-array) data model** — `{op: "hsl", bands: {red: {hue,saturation,luminance}, ...}}` — so a per-band UI control (`upsertHslBand(stack, bandName, patch)`) can patch exactly one band's values without threading the other 21 through, mirroring why masks needed their own CRUD instead of `opValue`/`upsertOp`'s scalar shape.
- **UI: flat per-band groups, no 3-tab (Hue/Saturation/Luminance) structure this slice** — real Lightroom's own HSL panel is 3 tabs over the same 8 bands, but no tab-widget precedent exists anywhere in this codebase, and building one purely for panel layout (not for any missing capability) would be new-pattern scope unjustified by this slice alone. All 8 bands × all 3 parameters genuinely work; only navigation/grouping differs from real Lightroom, matching this project's "smallest real instance first" discipline without cutting real functionality. Each band gets a small color swatch (`hsl(center, 70%, 50%)`, zero new math — the angle is already a shared constant) since "Aqua" vs. "Blue" vs. "Purple" are genuinely ambiguous by name alone.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit): dragged the real Red band's Hue/Saturation/Luminance sliders, confirmed the real persisted `hsl` op via `getEditStack`; confirmed the WGSL pipeline renders without error with non-identity bands active; ran a real export and pixel-sampled two real regions of the source photo against the true unedited source file (not a downscaled preview, to avoid a resolution-mismatch confound) — a reddish-brown region (hue ~21°, near the Red/Orange boundary) shifted from `srgb(53,35,25)` to `srgb(141,106,52)` (a strong, correct shift matching the boosted Red band), while a yellow-green region (hue ~97°, well outside Red's influence) came back **byte-identical** to the unedited baseline, `srgb(133,147,124)` both before and after — clean, decisive proof of band isolation, not a global shift. One real confound caught and corrected along the way, not silently worked around: the first pass showed the "unaffected" region shifting too, traced to leftover `exposure`/mask ops from an earlier, unrelated session's own empirical testing still sitting in the real catalog (not something this slice introduced) — isolated by temporarily clearing those ops for a clean comparison, then restored to their original state afterward.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 99/99 passing (4 new hand-derived tests, expected values computed via script running the exact same formula: absent bands is an exact passthrough; a pixel exactly at a band's own hue center isolates that band completely; a boundary-hue pixel with opposite-signed neighboring-band luminance deltas proves a smooth blend rather than a hard nearest-band cutoff — the near-cancellation this test's specific values were chosen to produce would look very different under a cutoff bug; a near-gray pixel proves `chroma_fade` measurably suppresses the shift).

## M3 — Tone Curve: master RGB point curve (2026-08-04)

Next unbuilt M3 scope item after all mask work: "Tone curve, HSL/color mixer, split toning, dehaze, clarity/texture, vignette, grain" — scoped to Tone Curve only, matching this project's own "smallest real instance first" pattern and `DevelopPanel.svelte`'s own placeholder (`{ title: "Tone Curve", note: "Point curve editor" }`), which this slice replaces with a real implementation. A single master curve (applied identically to R/G/B, not separate per-channel tabs — a named follow-up) mapping input tonal value to output, edited by dragging points on a graph. Real Lightroom applies Tone Curve after Basic-panel exposure/contrast/saturation, before HSL — this app's simplified pipeline now runs exposure → contrast → saturation → **tone curve**, as a new final step in the existing global pass, before masks (which already read the post-global `rgb` as their accumulator base) layer on top.

- **Design reviewed before implementation, specifically on the one genuinely hard part**: the monotonic cubic interpolation algorithm. Chose Fritsch–Carlson (no overshoot/ringing between control points, unlike a naive natural cubic spline, which can produce visibly reversed-tone banding). The review wrote out the exact, correct 4-step algorithm precisely because one step (sign-zeroing a tangent at a local extremum, distinct from the later magnitude-only rescale step) is easy to omit and get subtly wrong — a plain averaged tangent can have the wrong *sign* at an extremum, which only that explicit zeroing step catches.
- **Parity mechanism: both the WGSL shader and `develop_engine.rs` build and consume the IDENTICAL 256-sample LUT**, not "Rust exact, shader approximate" — the latter would reintroduce a real near-extrema gap outside this module's existing `±2/255` test tolerance (established for ordinary float rounding, not for two structurally different evaluation methods). Both sides run the same Fritsch–Carlson tangent computation, sample at the same 256 points, and linearly interpolate between LUT entries the same way — parity by construction, not by luck. The curve-editor UI draws from this exact same LUT too, so what's shown always matches what's applied.
- **Shared math lives in `develop.js`** (`buildToneCurveLut`/`sampleCurveLut`, plus `getToneCurvePoints`/`upsertToneCurve` — a structured `{points: [{x,y}...]}` payload doesn't fit `opValue`/`upsertOp`'s scalar-value shape, the same reason masks needed their own CRUD helpers), consumed by both `DevelopCanvas.svelte` (GPU upload) and the new `ToneCurveEditor.svelte` (drawing).
- **New WGSL uniform** `@binding(5) curveLut: array<vec4<f32>, 64>` — packed as 64 vec4s (256 f32 samples), not `array<f32,256>`: WGSL's uniform-array stride must be a multiple of 16 bytes (the `Mask` struct's own comment already documents hitting this exact footgun once before). Device-scoped like `uniformBuffer`/`masksBuffer` (created once in `initGpu`, rewritten via `writeBuffer` on every render, never recreated per image) — unlike `sourceTexture`/`brushTextureArray`, which a curve's shape has nothing to do with.
- **`develop_engine.rs`** gets a hand-synced Rust port of the identical algorithm (`compute_tangents`/`hermite_at`/`build_curve_lut`/`sample_lut`), inserted into `apply_edit_stack` as the pipeline's new final global step, built once per call (not per pixel).
- **New `ToneCurveEditor.svelte`**: an SVG point-curve graph. Click empty space to add a point (rejected past `MAX_CURVE_POINTS=16`, and rejected within a minimum x-spacing of an existing point — a real, guarded-against divide-by-zero risk in the secant-slope computation both this component and the Rust/WGSL sides share); drag a point (endpoints locked to x=0/x=1, interior points clamped strictly between their neighbors so a drag can never cross/reorder points mid-gesture); double-click an interior point to delete it (endpoints are permanent anchors). Drag interaction mirrors `DevelopCanvas.svelte`'s existing mask-handle-drag ordering exactly — set drag state *before* calling `setPointerCapture`, wrapped in try/catch — since that specific ordering is a real, previously-hit bug in this exact class of interaction (a throw from `setPointerCapture` called first silently aborted everything after it), not a hypothetical worth re-deriving from scratch.
- **A real accessibility gap caught before shipping, fixed rather than suppressed**: `svelte-check`'s a11y lint flagged the graph's click-to-add background and the draggable points as mouse-only interactive elements with no keyboard path. This codebase's own established practice is a real fix, not a lint-suppressing comment (no `svelte-ignore` precedent exists anywhere in this codebase, confirmed by grep) — added `role`/`tabindex` plus real keyboard support: Enter/Space on the graph adds a point at the curve's own current midpoint value; Arrow Up/Down (Shift for a bigger step) nudge a focused point's y; Delete/Backspace removes an interior point, mirroring the mouse double-click.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit): dispatched real `PointerEvent`s/`MouseEvent`s against the real `ToneCurveEditor` SVG, confirmed a real added point and a real dragged point both persisted correctly via `getEditStack` (a genuine test-timing bug in the harness itself was caught and fixed along the way — the first pass read back too early, before the existing 250ms persistence debounce had fired, and looked exactly like a real delete-not-working bug until the wait was extended past 250ms and the delete showed up correctly); confirmed the WGSL pipeline renders with no error overlay with a real non-identity 3-point curve active; confirmed adding points past `MAX_CURVE_POINTS` is silently capped at exactly 16, not runaway; confirmed double-clicking an interior point removes exactly one point and double-clicking an endpoint is correctly a no-op.
- **Not separately empirically re-verified via a live export**: the export path (`export.rs::export_one`) calls the exact same `develop_engine.rs::apply_edit_stack` function the new hand-derived Rust unit tests already exercise directly and exactly — that's the same code path, not an inference from a different one, so a live-app export pixel-check would have been redundant with what the unit tests already prove precisely.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings (including 0 a11y warnings, after the real fix above). `cargo test --lib`: 95/95 passing (3 new hand-derived tests: identity curve is an exact passthrough, a 2-point linear curve shifts each RGB channel independently through the identical function — proving this is a master curve, not a luma-weighted saturation-style adjustment — and a curve-plus-mask combination proving the curve is applied before the mask reads its accumulator).

## Develop: a true 1:1 preview tier for 100% zoom (2026-08-04)

User feedback: zooming to 100% in Develop just CSS-magnified the same `DEVELOP_PREVIEW_MAX_DIMENSION`-capped (2048px longest edge) draft preview — no genuinely finer pixel detail became visible, since the underlying decoded texture never changed. `PRD/PRD.md` already names the target architecture ("mirrors Lightroom's own Standard/1:1 Preview cache") — this closes that gap with a second, lazily-built cache tier.

- **Design reviewed before implementation.** It confirmed the core approach (2 tiers, lazy-build-on-first-zoom, matching `zoomMode`'s existing hard two-value `"fit"|"100"` enum — a 3rd tier would be dead code with no zoom state to attach to until continuous zoom exists) and found two real gaps to fold in: the existing `remove_images` command only cleaned up the draft tier's cached PNG on removal, leaving a new full-res tier as a permanent orphan; and no code anywhere checked `device.limits.maxTextureDimension2D` before creating a GPU texture, a real (if rare on realistic desktop hardware) risk once genuinely native-resolution decodes get uploaded.
- **Backend** (`preview_cache.rs`): new `ensure_develop_full_preview`/`ensure_develop_full_preview_for_hash`, mirroring the existing draft-tier pair exactly, reusing `source_decode::decode_preview` (already the function `export.rs` uses for full-res export — genuinely native resolution, no cap) with no resize step. Cache key `{content_hash}_full.png` — a distinct filename from the draft tier's `{content_hash}.png`, same "second content-hash-keyed artifact, distinct suffix" precedent as the edited-thumbnail cache. Deliberately **not** added to the background `pregenerate_missing` pass — built lazily, once, the first time an image is actually zoomed to 100%, matching real Lightroom's own 1:1-preview behavior and avoiding a 5-10x background-decode-cost multiplier for images that may never be zoomed into. New sibling Tauri command `get_develop_full_preview` (not a parameter on the existing `get_develop_preview` — matches this codebase's established one-function-per-concern split). `remove_images`'s cleanup extended to also delete the `_full.png` sibling if present.
- **Frontend** (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`): `loadImage`'s ~70-line GPU-resource-creation block (texture/brush-texture-array/bind-group/canvas-sizing) extracted into a shared `applyBitmapToGpu(bitmap)`, called both on image change and by a new `upgradeToFullTier` on tier swap — one implementation, not two that could drift. A new `$effect` (`status==="ready" && zoomMode==="100" && activeTier!=="full"`) triggers the upgrade for both existing zoom-to-100% entry points (canvas click-to-zoom, zoom-badge button) for free, since both just flip `zoomMode`. **Deliberately does not touch `status`** — the draft-resolution image stays visible and interactive the entire time the full tier decodes in the background (a progressive upgrade, not a blank "Decoding…" reload). Once upgraded, stays upgraded for the rest of that image's session (toggling back to "fit" doesn't downgrade), so `100%→fit→100%` never redecodes.
- **A real scroll-drift bug caught and fixed before it shipped**, not just accepted: the existing click-to-zoom centering math computed a native-pixel point valid for the draft tier's dimensions at that instant, but the full tier landing later resizes the canvas out from under that scroll position. Fixed by storing the click as a **normalized** (0-1) fraction (`lastZoomFocus`) instead — resolution-independent, so `upgradeToFullTier` re-applies the same fraction against the *new* canvas dimensions once the swap lands, keeping the originally-clicked point centered through the resolution upgrade.
- **Brush masks and the color-range eyedropper confirmed correct with zero special-casing**, not assumed: dab coordinates are stored normalized (0-1), so `applyBitmapToGpu`'s existing per-call `brushRasterState` reset (already required regardless, since `brushTextureArray` itself is destroyed/recreated) correctly re-rasterizes every mask's full dab history at the new resolution on the very next render. `sourceSampleCanvas` (the eyedropper's pixel source) is re-derived on every tier swap too, not deferred — leaving it stale at draft resolution while the GPU texture is full-res would have silently made color sampling less precise at exactly the moment the user zoomed in to inspect detail.
- **GPU texture-dimension safety**: `applyBitmapToGpu` reads `device.limits.maxTextureDimension2D` and downscales defensively via `createImageBitmap`'s own resize option if a bitmap (either tier) would exceed it — one code path for both tiers, an honest accepted degradation on hardware where it matters rather than an opaque WebGPU validation crash.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit, with incremental progress reporting added specifically because the first attempt appeared to hang — root-caused to a stale disk cache from an earlier interrupted run giving a misleadingly-instant "warm cache hit" result; deleted the cache file and re-ran for a genuine cold-decode test): against the real 4032×3024 JPEG test image, confirmed the draft tier (2048×1536, correctly capped) loads and stays visibly displayed 662ms after zooming to 100% with no "Decoding…" overlay present; confirmed the full tier eventually lands at the real native resolution (4032×3024, matching the source file exactly) and `get_develop_full_preview` returns a real file on disk at that path/size; confirmed toggling 100%→fit→100% re-enters at native resolution near-instantly (117ms, no observable redecode) rather than re-fetching. One test-scaffolding artifact worth naming honestly: an absolute "elapsed since click" timestamp captured mid-test came back nonsensically large (webview timer behavior while the window was backgrounded across the harness's own real-world pauses, not a real multi-hour decode) — the functional assertions above are what were actually verified, not that specific number.
- **Not empirically re-exercised**: the `remove_images` cleanup extension for the `_full.png` sibling — doing so would have required deleting the user's real, only cataloged test image from their real catalog to test removal. Verified by code correctness instead (mirrors the already-tested existing draft-tier cleanup pattern exactly, same file).
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 92/92 passing (4 new full-tier parity tests in `preview_cache.rs`, real-file-gated, mirroring the draft tier's own existing test shapes: cached separately from the draft tier with the correct distinguishing suffix, cache-hit skips redecode, same-content-different-path dedupes).

## Color range mask: re-sample the reference color (2026-08-04)

User feedback: color-range masks had no way to change the sampled reference color after creation — the swatch in `MaskEditorPanel.svelte` was deliberately non-interactive at the time, with an explicit "delete and recreate instead" scope cut in its own doc comment. Replaced that with a real re-sample action, reusing the existing click-to-sample plumbing (`sampleSourcePixel`/`screenToNormalized`/the color-range pointer flow in `DevelopCanvas.svelte`) rather than building a second sampling mechanism.

- **New eyedropper button next to the swatch** (`MaskEditorPanel.svelte`), same icon as the Color Range tool button. Clicking it sets `activeTool = "color_range"` and a new `colorRangeResampleTarget` state (`+page.svelte`) to the selected mask's id; the next canvas click reuses the exact same commit path that CREATES a mask, except `DevelopCanvas.svelte`'s pointerup handler now branches on a new `colorRangeResampleId` prop — if set, it calls a new `onColorRangeResampled(id, refColor)` callback that patches the existing mask's `refColor` via `updateMask`, instead of `onMaskCreated` appending a new one.
- **Toggle symmetry with the tool-strip's own `onToolToggle`**: clicking the eyedropper again while already resampling cancels it (mirrors clicking an already-active tool button a second time). A derived `isResamplingColor` (`colorRangeResampleTarget !== null && colorRangeResampleTarget === selectedMaskId`) drives both the button's active-state styling and its tooltip text.
- **Self-cleaning resample state, not patched into every reset site**: rather than manually clearing `colorRangeResampleTarget` at every place `activeTool`/`selectedMaskId` can change (tool-strip toggle, panel close, mask delete, module switch, selecting a different mask chip...), a small `$effect` clears it whenever the color-range tool stops being active OR the selection moves away from the resample target — the only two conditions under which the target is still valid. Deliberately does **not** also force `activeTool = null` from inside that effect — doing so would clobber a user's very next tool selection (e.g. clicking Brush while a stale resample was pending would get silently reverted back to no-tool by this effect running right after), verified as a real risk before ruling it out, not just asserted.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used throughout this project, removed before commit — real dispatched `PointerEvent`s against the real canvas, real IPC round-trips against the real catalog): created a color-range mask by clicking a known point, confirmed its persisted `refColor` matched that point's real pixel value (cross-checked via ImageMagick against the real decoded preview PNG); entered resample mode and clicked a different point, confirmed the SAME mask's `refColor` updated to the new point's real pixel value with the mask count unchanged (no duplicate created) and resample mode correctly exited afterward; re-entered resample mode and cancelled via the toggle button instead of clicking canvas, confirmed no mutation occurred; re-entered resample mode and deselected the mask instead, confirmed the self-cleaning effect cleared the target while correctly leaving `activeTool` alone. One real false alarm during this pass, root-caused rather than assumed: the first run showed identical before/after colors, which turned out to be residual `color_range_mask` entries left in the real catalog from an earlier slice's own empirical verification pass (not cleaned up at the time) — `.find()` was grabbing the wrong (older) mask by op-type alone. Fixed the test harness to use the last match (matching `addMask`'s append-only order), confirmed clean after clearing the residue — not a bug in the shipped feature.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 89/89 passing, unchanged (no Rust logic touched — this is a pure preview-interaction slice; re-sampling doesn't change the persisted op shape, `develop_engine.rs` needs no changes).

## UI polish: mask tool strip switched from text to icon buttons (2026-08-03)

User feedback: the 5 mask-kind buttons (Linear/Radial/Brush/Luminance Range/Color Range) plus brush options plus a variable-length mask-chip list were all crowding one row as mask kinds accumulated. Switched the 5 tool buttons in `MaskToolStrip.svelte` from text labels to small hand-drawn SVG icons (line/circle geometry echoing each mask kind's own on-canvas look — e.g. two dots + a line for linear gradient, concentric circles for radial, a slanted rect + dab for brush, a black-to-white gradient bar for luminance range, an eyedropper for color range), each still carrying its existing `title` tooltip plus a new `aria-label` (the icon itself is `aria-hidden`, so the accessible name doesn't disappear along with the visible text). Erase/New Brush and the mask-chip list are unchanged (out of scope — user asked specifically about the mask tools). `.tool.icon` is a new CSS variant (fixed 28×26px, centered) layered on the existing `.tool` base styling, so the existing text-button styling (`.tool.small` for Erase/New Brush) is untouched. `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. Not run through the native app (no screenshot tool for it in this environment) — hand-verified icon geometry (small integer coordinates in a 16×16 viewBox) instead, consistent with this project's honest-about-verification-limits practice elsewhere.

## Current phase: M3 — Local adjustments & non-destructive toolkit — IN PROGRESS (started 2026-07-30)

M1 and M2 are both done (see below). M2's scope was expanded on 2026-07-29 (user request) with four items pulled to the front of M3 before its "real" local-adjustment work: a general Settings dialog, completing the Library quick-action area with keyboard shortcuts, basic Develop pan/zoom, and extracting the develop engine (consolidating the WGSL-shader/CPU-export duplication) — see PRD/MILESTONES.md's M3 scope list for the full reasoning. **All four front-loaded slices are done (1–4), all three mask types (5 linear, 6 radial, 7 brush) are done, a mask UI polish pass landed (overlay + feather indicators), and both range-masking slices (luminance range, color range) are done — "Range masking: color range and luminance range selection" is now fully shipped.** MILESTONES.md's "adjustment brush (with auto-mask)" bullet is paint/erase-complete; auto-mask remains deliberately deferred. **Tone Curve, HSL / Color Mixer, Split Toning, Dehaze, Clarity & Texture, Vignette, Grain, and now Sharpening & Noise Reduction are all done too** — see below, and **the entire Basic/Detail/Effects adjustment surface (every non-local-adjustment slider in PRD §7.4/MILESTONES.md's M3 list) is now fully shipped**. Dehaze was the first op needing a real multi-pass GPU render graph (8 pipelines, not 1) and a restructured CPU pipeline (multiple buffer passes, not one loop) — a real architectural step up from every prior per-pixel-only op, deliberately scoped as its own slice-1 (percentile-gated atmospheric light, a real guided filter, and reduced-internal-resolution computation for smoother 100%-zoom interactivity all explicitly named and deferred to a slice 2). Clarity & Texture reused that same architecture directly (Texture -> Clarity -> Dehaze, all three now sharing one dirty-key-gated spatial-ops block) rather than inventing a new one. Vignette and Grain, by contrast, needed none of that machinery — both pure per-pixel effects, they fold directly into the existing final pass/loop every op before Dehaze already used, each with its own small dedicated uniform buffer. Sharpening & Noise Reduction went full Lightroom depth (user's explicit choice, 7 controls across 3 ops) and needed real new multi-pass blur work again (6 more GPU passes, reusing the established separable-box-mean primitive at three different radii) plus a genuinely new WGSL pattern (a uniform-driven dynamic loop bound for Sharpening's own user-controlled Radius, unlike every other op's fixed compile-time radius) — a design review caught a real cache-invalidation bug tied to that new radius control before it ever shipped. **Crop & Straighten (full interactive tool, user's explicit choice) is now done too** — the biggest slice of the milestone by a wide margin, but architecturally the SIMPLEST of the last five in one specific sense: after a design review flagged real problems with an initial GPU-render-graph-based draft, it shipped with ZERO WGSL/GPU changes at all, using a pure CSS transform+clip trick for the live preview and keeping the real pixel-level rotate+crop entirely in Rust, shared by export and thumbnail regen. **History/Undo/Snapshots (full scope: undo/redo + a visual History panel + named Snapshots, user's explicit choice) is now done too** — see above. **Presets (full scope: create/save/apply + batch-apply across Library + import/export as files, user's explicit choice) is now done too** — see above. Next: lens corrections per PRD §7.4/MILESTONES.md — the last unbuilt item in M3's scope list.

## M3 — Range masking, part 2: color range mask (2026-08-03)

Second and last half of "Range masking: color range and luminance range selection" (PRD/MILESTONES.md), following the luminance-range slice (PR #31). That slice explicitly deferred color range, naming two real reasons: it needs a genuinely new click-to-sample-a-pixel-color interaction, and it needs an `ImageBitmap`-retention fix its own design review confirmed as real (`DevelopCanvas.svelte`'s decoded bitmap was discarded right after its one-time GPU-texture upload, with nothing keeping pixel data around for a future eyedropper to read).

- **Design reviewed before implementation, and it caught a real, would-have-shipped correctness bug** in the weight formula's first draft: at `range=0, feather=0` — the single most obvious "strict color match" setting a user would reach for — the formula evaluated to weight=0 even at the exact sampled pixel, because the `denom` floor meant to prevent divide-by-zero at `feather=0` also diluted the numerator's `feather_width` term. Fixed by reformulating so `denom` is the transition width added *after* the threshold (`weight = clamp((threshold - dist) / denom + 1.0, 0.0, 1.0)`), not blended into the numerator — `dist <= threshold` now always yields exactly 1 regardless of how small `denom` is. The review also flagged three more sites sharing the exact "untyped fallback assumes linear" pattern already shipped and fixed once for the mask-packing loop (`MaskEditorPanel.svelte`'s title ternary, `MaskToolStrip.svelte`'s chip-label fallback, `+page.svelte`'s `handleMaskCreated` dispatch) — all four given their own explicit branch before the fallback, not appended after it.
- **New `color_range_mask` op**: `{id, refColor: {r,g,b} (0-1 floats), range, feather, invert, exposure, contrast, saturation}` — single-sided trapezoidal falloff in RGB-distance space (closer to `radial_mask_weight`'s inside/outside shape than luminance range's two-sided band, since color range has one reference point and one tolerance, not two edges), `max_dist = sqrt(3)` (the RGB unit cube's own diagonal). Packs into the *existing* 12-float `Mask` struct layout with zero new fields — `start_end.xyz` = refColor, `start_end.w` = range (linear and radial masks already use all four `start_end` slots too, so this isn't new territory for the struct). `Mask::weight`'s `rgb` parameter (added specifically for luminance range) is reused directly — no signature change needed, and order-dependency (a preceding mask's effect on `rgb` shifts what a later color-range mask selects) carries over for free, for the same already-documented WYSIWYG reason.
- **Pixel sampling — retained via a persistent `OffscreenCanvas`, not a GPU texture readback**: `loadImage`'s decoded bitmap is now also drawn once into a new `sourceSampleCanvas`/`sourceSampleCtx` (recreated per image, same pattern `brushTextureArray` already uses), queryable via `sampleSourcePixel(normX, normY)`. `bitmap.close()` moved to the very end of `loadImage` (after every remaining `bitmap.width`/`.height` read) rather than right after the draw — closing a bitmap zeroes its width/height per spec, and the brush-texture-array sizing and canvas-dimension assignment both still needed to read those after the sample-canvas draw. **Deliberate, named simplification**: samples the original decoded SOURCE pixel, not the currently-graded preview — a true WYSIWYG eyedropper would need a live GPU texture readback (`copyTextureToBuffer`/`mapAsync`), deferred as real future scope, not silently accepted as permanent.
- **A fourth, genuinely distinct mask-creation interaction pattern, named as such**: linear/radial drag two points, luminance range creates on tool-button click with no canvas interaction, brush paints continuous strokes — color range is a single click on the canvas that samples a pixel and immediately commits. Click-vs-accidental-drag disambiguation reuses the same `DRAG_CLICK_THRESHOLD` pattern already established for the zoom-toggle click, rather than committing unconditionally on pointerdown.
- **WGSL dispatch**: one more ascending band (`else if (kind < 3.5)` for luminance range, bare `else` for color range/kind 4) — following exactly the pattern the luminance-range slice's own comment named as the one to follow for a future 5th kind.
- **UI**: `MaskToolStrip.svelte` gained a Color Range tool button (routed through `activeTool`, unlike luminance range's direct-create button — this kind genuinely needs canvas click coordinates) and a 5th independent chip counter. `MaskEditorPanel.svelte` gained a dedicated block (non-interactive color swatch showing the sampled reference color, Range slider, Feather slider) — re-sampling an existing mask's color is explicitly out of scope, matching real Lightroom's own "click again to re-sample" model.
- **A real bug found during empirical verification — in the TEST HARNESS, not the product.** The first live-app pass showed the click-to-sample interaction silently doing nothing (`activeTool` never reset, no mask created). Root-caused via temporary diagnostics added directly inside `DevelopCanvas.svelte`'s click handler (reported through `report_spike_result`, since WKWebView's console isn't forwarded to this environment): `sourceSampleCanvas`/`sourceSampleCtx` were both `false` at click time. The harness's own "wait for the canvas to be ready" check (`canvasEl.width > 0`) was broken — the `<canvas>` element exists in the DOM immediately on mount with the browser's *default* 300×150 dimensions, so that check was always true well before `loadImage()` had even started, let alone populated the sample canvas. Fixed the harness (not the product) to wait for `.zoom-badge` instead, which only renders once `status === "ready"`. A second, unrelated harness artifact (`fourKindsCoexist` failing) turned out to be leftover masks accumulated across several earlier harness re-runs against the same real cataloged image, pushing it to the 8-mask cap — fixed by explicitly resetting the edit stack to empty at the top of the harness before each run. Neither issue required any product-code change.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged technique used for the two prior slices, removed before commit): clicked the real sky-blue region of the loaded source image and confirmed the persisted mask's `refColor` matched an independent ImageMagick sample of the same source file almost exactly (`(162,210,248)` sampled vs. `(162,210,248)` real); confirmed a small accidental drag while the tool was active does NOT create a mask; confirmed the mask coexists with luminance-range and linear-gradient masks in one stack with no render error; confirmed Range/Feather slider changes persist via `getEditStack`; confirmed the "O" hotkey toggles the overlay; real export with a strong exposure boost (+3EV) on the color-range mask targeting the sky-blue region — the sky blew to pure white while a distinctly-colored control region (real brown lower-left pixel, well outside the color-distance threshold) came back byte-identical to the source, real proof the effect is color-selective, not just "did something."
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 89/89 passing (4 new color-range parity cases: the exact-match-at-zero-feather regression test for the bug the review caught, a far-pixel-gets-no-effect case, a feathered-halfway-blend case, and an order-dependency case mirroring luminance range's own — each hand-computed before writing the assertion, same discipline as every prior mask slice).

## M3 — Range masking, part 1: luminance range mask (2026-08-03)

Next unbuilt M3 scope item after the mask-overlay polish pass: "Range masking: color range and luminance range selection" (PRD/MILESTONES.md). Split into two slices, same way linear/radial split before it — this slice is luminance range only; color range (needing a genuinely new click-to-sample-a-pixel-color interaction) is a deliberate, named follow-up, not bundled in.

This is the **first mask kind whose weight depends on pixel VALUE (luminance) rather than pixel POSITION** — every earlier kind (linear/radial: pure geometry; brush: rasterized from geometry) computed weight from `uv`/`aspect` alone. Turns out architecturally simpler in one way (no new texture, no new geometry — the fragment shader already has `rgb` in scope before the mask loop even starts), but surfaces a genuinely new question (mask order now affects *what gets selected*, not just how it's graded) and forced two real, pre-existing latent bugs into the open.

- **Design reviewed before implementation, and it found two real, would-have-shipped bugs in the EXISTING code** (not just the new feature): the WGSL shader's mask-kind dispatch (`if params.z > 1.5 {brush} else if params.z < 0.5 {linear} else {radial}`) is a two-comparison threshold chain that only correctly bucketed kinds 0/1/2 by *coincidence* — a 4th kind would silently alias onto the brush branch, sampling a texture layer that was never assigned one. And the JS mask-packing loop's catch-all `else` (which assumed "anything that isn't radial or brush is linear") would throw a `TypeError` reading `m.start.x` on `undefined` the instant a luminance-range mask coexisted with any other kind, aborting the render for every mask in the stack, not just the new one. Both fixed: the WGSL dispatch became real ascending bands (ordered `if`/`else if` bounding one kind each, not overlapping thresholds); the JS loop gained an explicit branch per kind with no assumption-based catch-all.
- **New `luminance_range_mask` op**: `{id, rangeMin, rangeMax, feather, invert, exposure, contrast, saturation}`, `rangeMin`/`rangeMax`/`feather` stored 0-100 (matching linear/radial's own `feather` convention, not a separate 0-1 scale). Weight formula (verified independently by the review, same "raw expression, clamp exactly once" style as `mask_weight`/`radial_mask_weight`): `weight = clamp(min(rising, falling), 0, 1)` where `rising`/`falling` are the two UNCLAMPED slope expressions — taking `min()` before the single final clamp is what keeps this correct outside the range (a naive per-term-clamped version would incorrectly clamp a far-outside luma back toward 1 instead of 0). Packs into the *existing* 12-float `Mask` struct layout with zero new fields — even less new uniform surface than brush needed.
- **Mask order now affects selection, not just grading — named explicitly and given its own parity test**, not left implicit. Because luminance weight reads `rgb`, and `rgb` is a mutating accumulator in both the WGSL loop and `apply_edit_stack`'s Rust loop, a luminance-range mask's effective selection now depends on which masks precede it in the stack — the correct WYSIWYG behavior (select pixels as currently graded), a genuinely new category of interaction no earlier mask kind had. `Mask::weight`'s Rust signature grew from `(&self, uv, aspect)` to `(&self, uv, aspect, rgb)` to support this.
- **The mask-overlay mechanism from the prior slice was unified, not duplicated a second time**: instead of building a separate overlay path for luminance range (no texture to sample — its weight is analytic), the brush-only `overlay_layer`/`overlay_invert` uniform pair was replaced with a single `selected_mask_index`, and the overlay blend moved *inside* the main per-mask loop, reusing each mask's own already-computed, already-inverted `weight` — gated to `kind > 1.5` (brush and luminance range) so linear/radial correctly keep their prior dashed-outline-only scope. This is a real simplification (less code, no separate re-sample-and-re-invert step) that also means any future no-geometry mask kind gets overlay support for free. `showBrushOverlay` renamed to `showMaskOverlay` throughout to match its now-general scope; a new `OVERLAY_CAPABLE_MASK_OPS` allowlist in `develop.js` is the single place this list lives (hotkey gate + checkbox gate can't drift apart).
- **UI**: luminance range creates immediately on tool-button click, with **no canvas interaction at all** — a real, deliberate divergence from every other mask kind (it has no geometry to place, matching real Lightroom's own behavior for this tool). This doesn't fit the existing `onMaskCreated(placement)` funnel (which is entirely driven by `DevelopCanvas.svelte`'s own pointer handlers), so `MaskToolStrip.svelte`'s button calls a new, explicitly-separate `onCreateLuminanceRange` callback rather than forcing a fake "click anywhere to commit nothing" interaction just to reuse the existing path. `MaskEditorPanel.svelte` gained a dedicated Min/Max/Feather block (the shared Feather row means something different for this kind — a band width around two edges, not one boundary) with a cheap `linear-gradient(black, white)` CSS track background standing in for a real luminance histogram — closes the "what does 30 even mean" gap without building histogram UI from scratch.
- **A real bug found during empirical verification — in the TEST, not the product.** The first order-dependency verification (real image, linear mask at -3EV then a luminance mask at +3EV) showed *zero* visible effect and looked exactly like a real regression. Root-caused via an isolated Rust test (bypassing Tauri/IPC/catalog entirely, applying the exact same op stack to the exact same real image): both masks WERE being applied correctly, but `-3EV` then `+3EV` are exact mathematical inverses (`2^-3 × 2^3 = 1`) — two full-weight opposite-EV masks exactly cancel, producing a result byte-identical to "neither mask applied at all." Not a product bug; a test-design mistake. Fixed by using non-canceling exposure values (`-3.0` then `+1.0`), re-verified against a hand-computed expected value (`~(41,53,62)`) and confirmed via the standalone Rust test *before* re-running the full empirical pass — the real fix (`develop_engine.rs`'s `Mask::weight`/`apply_edit_stack`) needed no changes at all.
- **Verified empirically through the real running app** (same `report_spike_result`-bridged harness technique used for the two prior slices, removed before commit): confirmed the packing-loop crash the review found is actually fixed — all four mask kinds (linear/radial/brush/luminance range) coexisting in one stack renders without throwing; confirmed real persisted defaults (30/70/20) and real updates via `getEditStack`; confirmed the "O" hotkey toggles the overlay when a luminance-range mask is selected and correctly does nothing when a radial mask is selected instead (the preserved-scope gate); real export with a luminance-range mask (+2EV, range [35,55]) against real source pixels — a bright point (~79% luma, outside the range) came back byte-identical to the source, a midtone point (~41% luma, inside the range) came back blown to white, exactly as the range boundaries predict; real export proving order-dependency on the actual multi-pixel image (not just the 1×1 unit test), landing within rounding of the hand-computed expected pixel.
- `npm run check`: 0 errors/warnings. `cargo test --lib`: 85/85 passing (5 new luminance-range parity cases: below-range, inside-range, above-range, a feathered-edge blend, and the order-dependency case — each hand-computed before writing the assertion, same discipline as every prior mask slice; the temporary debug test used to root-cause the empirical false-alarm was deleted before commit).

## Mask UI polish — brush overlay + feather range indicators (2026-08-03)

User feedback on the just-merged brush/gradient masks (PR #29): brush masks were invisible until a nonzero adjustment was dialed in (unlike linear/radial's dashed outline), and linear/radial only showed their *unfeathered* boundary, not the actual feathered transition range. Scope confirmed directly with the user: the colored-overlay ask is brush-only; the feather-range ask applies to both linear and radial.
